Yoneda Lemma for 𝒟 \mathcal{D} -Simplicial Spaces
Nima Rasekh

Abstract
. For a small category 𝒟 \mathcal{D} we define fibrations of simplicial presheaves on the category 𝒟 × Δ \mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}} , which we call localized 𝒟 \mathcal{D} -left fibration . We show these fibrations can be seen as fibrant objects in a model structure, the localized 𝒟 \mathcal{D} -covariant model structure , that is Quillen equivalent to a category of functors valued in simplicial presheaves on 𝒟 \mathscr{D} , where the Quillen equivalence is given via a generalization of the Grothendieck construction . We use our understanding of this construction to give a detailed characterization of fibrations and weak equivalences in this model structure and in particular obtain a Yoneda lemma. We apply this general framework to study Cartesian fibrations of ( ∞ , n ) (\infty,n) -categories, for models of ( ∞ , n ) (\infty,n) -categories that arise via simplicial presheaves, such as n n -fold complete Segal spaces . This, in particular, results in the Yoneda lemma and Grothendieck construction for Cartesian fibrations of ( ∞ , n ) (\infty,n) -categories.



0.
Introduction

0.1.
(\infty,1)
-Categories and Their Fibrations

The theory of
(\infty,1)
-categories
(also known as the theory of
\infty
-categories
or simply
homotopy theories
) has proven very effective in using categorical techniques to study homotopical phenomena and we can see its effect in all branches of homotopy theory from
chromatic homotopy theory

[
MS16
]
to
algebraic K-theory

[
GGN15
]
to
derived geometry

[
Lur18
]
to even
homotopy type theory

[
GK17
]
.

Unfortunately, such theoretical benefits do come with a price: Any notion of category that enables us to study objects that arise in homotopy theory needs to be suitably flexible and weak. In particular, in the
(\infty,1)
-categorical world any classical categorical notion such as associativity and functoriality needs to be defined in a suitably weak and “up to homotopy" manner to have the desired applications.

As a result it is often quite challenging (and sometimes even impossible) to define a functor of
(\infty,1)
-categories directly as each homotopy used to specify a functor necessitates a higher homotopy creating a never-ending cycle of “homotopies between homotopies". Fortunately, classical category theory already provides us with an alternative method to study many important functors that arise in category theory. In order to understand it we need to review the role of
fibrations
in category theory.

Grothendieck, and more generally Bourbaki, introduce what is now known as
discrete Grothendieck opfibrations
which are functors of categories that satisfy certain lifting conditions and then show that the now called
Grothendieck construction

[
gro03
]
gives rise to an equivalence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{C}}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\mathrm{op}\mathscr{G}\mathrm{roth}_{/\mathscr{C}},

between functors valued in in
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
and discrete Grothendieck opfibrations.

This approach to functors has been generalized to the
(\infty,1)
-categorical setting in various steps. The first were taken by Joyal using the most prominent model of
(\infty,1)
-categories (in the sense of
[
Toë05
]
),
quasi-categories

[
BV73
]
. He introduced the notion of
left fibrations
of quasi-categories and studied their relations to functors valued in spaces
[
Joy08a
,
Joy08b
]
.

A next big step was taken by Lurie
[
Lur09a
]
, who again used quasi-categories to define
coCartesian fibrations
and prove an equivalence between coCartesian fibrations and functors valued in quasi-categories, by constructing a Quillen equivalence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}^{+}_{/S})^{Cart}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathfrak{C}[S]^{op},(s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}^{+})^{Cart})^{proj}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{+}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Un}^{+}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

of the relevant model structures
[
Lur09a
, Theorem 3.2.0.1]
, which in particular restricts to an equivalence between left fibrations and functors valued in spaces
[
Lur09a
, Theorem 2.2.1.2]
. Using Cartesian fibrations and the straightening construction he then studies many important categorical constructions, such as
adjunctions

[
Lur09a
, Section 5.2]
,
\infty
-toposes

[
Lur09a
, Chapter 6]
,
symmetric monoidal structures
and
\infty
-operads

[
Lur17
, Chapter 2]
. As a very elegant example, for a given quasi-category
S
and object
x
, there is no direct way to construct a
corepresentable functor

\mathrm{Map}_{S}(x,-)
, given that composition is only defined up to homotopy. However, the corresponding right fibration, by
[
Lur09a
, Theorem 2.2.1.2]
, is the under-quasi-category
S_{x/}\to S
, which can be constructed very easily
[
Lur09a
, Proposition 1.2.9.3]
.

In the years since the original result by Lurie appeared, there have been many generalizations and alternative perspectives on the
(\infty,1)
-categorical Grothendieck construction. Heuts and Moerdijk present an alternative construction to the Grothendieck construction for left fibrations to the one given by Lurie also in the context of quasi-categories, which has the benefit that it is very explicit and easy to follow over strict categories
[
HM15
,
HM16
]
. Another construction of the straightening construction for left fibrations, also in the context of quasi-categories can be found in the work of Stevenson
[
Ste17
]
. There is also an analysis of left fibrations via the
universal left fibration
due to Cisinski
[
Cis19
]
, using ideas from
Cisinski model categories

[
Cis06
]
, whose results have been expanded by Nguyen to also apply to the coCartesian model structure
[
Ngu19
]
.

Parallel to the development of left and coCartesian fibrations for quasi-categories, there has also been a development of fibrations for
complete Segal spaces

[
Rez01
]
, which is another model of
(\infty,1)
-categories. This in particular includes work by deBrito
[
BdB18
]
and Kazhdan and Varshavsky
[
KV14
]
, as well as
[
Ras17b
]
.

Last but not least, there has also been a model-independent study of coCartesian fibrations. The work by Mazel-Gee
[
MG19b
,
MG19a
]
, while relying on quasi-categories, focuses on universal properties rather than specific model structure to study Cartesian fibrations and the Grothendieck construction. Similarly, the work by Ayala and Francis
[
AF20
]
, although technically in the context of quasi-categories, relies primarily on the universal properties of
exponentiable fibrations
. A more precise model-independent approach is due to Riehl and Verity, who introduced the model-independent notion of an
\infty
-cosmos

[
RV21
]
and use it to study fibrations and the Yoneda lemma
[
RV17
]
, which can then be applied to the models where fibrations had been studied already, such as quasi-categories and complete Segal spaces, but also models where it had not been studied, such as
Segal categories

[
HS98
,
Ber07
]
and
marked simplicial sets

[
Ver08
,
Lur09a
]
(see
[
Ber10
,
Ber18
]
for a review of most of these models). Finally, left fibrations
[
RS17
]
and coCartesian fibrations
[
BW21
]
have also been studied in the context of
homotopy type theory

[
Uni13
]
, a new logical foundations to mathematics more amenable to homotopical thinking.

To summarize, there are now many different methods to effectively translate between functors and fibrations and these methods have been used to great effect in the study of homotopy theory.

0.2.
First Steps into the World of
(\infty,n)
-Categories

Coming back to classical categories, we often expand our definitions to be able to better understand certain categories and their properties. The common example is the category of small categories, which we often consider as a
2
-category

[
KS74
]
enabling us to include natural transformations as part of the data and hence study
equivalences of categories
rather than just isomorphisms. A similar line of thinking is used when studying the
2
-category of
elementary toposes
or
Grothendieck toposes

[
MLM94
,
Joh02a
,
Joh02b
]
.

Applying that same mindset to
(\infty,1)
-categories, we hence want a proper theory of
(\infty,2)
-categories
, which is the appropriate framework to study
(\infty,1)
-categories. In fact, thinking inductively, we rather want a proper theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories
. As a result, there is now a long list of models of
(\infty,n)
-categories, such as
n
-fold complete Segal spaces

[
Bar05
]
,
\Theta_{n}
-spaces

[
Rez10a
]
,
\Theta_{n}
-sets

[
Ara14
]
,
n
-complicial sets

[
Ver08
]
and
n
-comical sets

[
DKLS20
,
CKM20
]
(see
[
Ber11
]
for a review of many of these models). Moreover,
(\infty,n)
-categories have found interesting applications in
derived algebraic geometry

[
GR17a
,
GR17b
]
(via the models of
scaled simplicial sets

[
GHL20b
]
and comical sets) and
topological field theories

[
Lur09c
,
CS19
]
(via the model of
n
-fold complete Segal spaces).

However, our knowledge of
(\infty,n)
-categories is far more limited than
(\infty,1)
-categories. First of all, for a general
n
while we do know that some models coincide, such as
n
-fold complete Segal spaces,
\Theta_{n}
-spaces and
\Theta_{n}
-sets
[
Ara14
,
BR13a
,
BR20
]
, or complicial sets and comical sets
[
DKM21
]
, and even have a axiomatic characterization of
(\infty,n)
-categories
[
BSP11
]
, the general equivalence of all models is known only for
n=2

[
GHL19
,
Lur09b
]
. Even in that case most equivalences are given via very complicated zigzags of Quillen equivalences of model categories and constructing direct equivalences is an ongoing effort
[
BOR21
]
.

Moreover, even the most foundational categorical concepts of
(\infty,2)
-categories, such as
limits
, are currently being developed using marked (or scaled) simplicial sets
[
GHL20a
,
GHL21a
,
AG20
]
or
double
(\infty,1)
-categories

[
Mos20
,
CM20b
,
CM20a
,
MSV20
]
. The same challenges persist when looking at fibrations of
(\infty,n)
-categories. There are now interesting results for fibrations of
(\infty,2)
-categories using the model of scaled simplicial sets
[
Lur09b
,
HNP19
,
GHL21b
,
AGS20b
,
AGS20a
,
AGS21
]
, but no other models or higher dimensions.

One option would be to forgo any focus on specific models and approach
(\infty,n)
-categories from the perspective of enriched quasi-categories
[
GH15
,
Hin20
]
. Using this approach has resulted in enriched forms of the Yoneda lemma
[
Hin20
,
Ber20
]
, which then can be applied in particular to
(\infty,n)
-categories, although it still lacks a theory of fibrations. However, even if a theory Cartesian fibrations of enriched quasi-categories is developed, the enriched quasi-categorical approach does have certain drawbacks. First of all, developing a proper theory of enriched
\infty
-categories is incredibly challenging and remains complicated to understand. Moreover, many applications of
(\infty,n)
-categories (such as topological field theories) do in fact use concrete models (such as
n
-fold complete Segal spaces)
[
CS19
]
and we expect that the further study of the theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories, such as (lax) (co)limits and adjunctions does require study them using concrete models rather than enriched quasi-categories.

The goal of this work is to take an important step by studying fibrations for most models of
(\infty,n)
-categories that arise as simplicial presheaves. This includes
n
-fold complete Segal spaces, but also complete Segal spaces enriched over
\Theta_{n}
-spaces. In line with previous important results regarding fibrations, we in particular want to study the Yoneda lemma for
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibrations and a Grothendieck construction for
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibrations, generalizing the straightening construction from
(\infty,1)
-categories to the
(\infty,n)
-categorical setting.

0.3.
Generalizing to
\mathcal{D}
-Fibrations

Before we proceed to the results of this paper it is instructive to review the most general method for constructing of models of
(\infty,n)
-categories based on simplicial presheaves (
[
BR20
, Corollary 7.2]
, generalizing
[
Rez10a
,
Bar05
]
), which always proceeds in three steps:

(1)

Start with the
Kan model structure
on simplicial sets.

(2)

Choose the appropriate diagram category
\Theta_{k}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{n-k}
and give the category of simplicial presheaves the
injective model structure
.

(3)

Impose various relations: the
Segal condition
for composition, the
completeness condition
and, if necessary, the
discreteness conditions
. Obtain the final model structure via
Bousfield localization
.

We want to generalize these three steps from an absolute setting to a fibrational setting.

For the first step we need a fibrational notion of spaces. This is precisely obtained by studying left fibrations of simplicial spaces
[
Ras17b
]
, which is also reviewed in
Section

1.7
.

Our next step is to generalize left fibrations to diagram categories. Instead of restricting ourselves to specific diagrams, we take a more general approach:
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category. Then a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space is a simplicial presheaf
\mathcal{D}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}
. A
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is then simply an injective fibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
Y\to X
, such that for every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, the map of simplicial spaces
Y[d]\to X[d]
is a left fibration as studied in
[
Ras17b
]
(
2.1
).

This simple looking definition has far reaching implications. Concretely, it can be realized as fibrant objects in a model structure on an over-category (
2.12
), which has many desirable properties (such as
invariance under level-wise complete Segal space equivalences
(
2.18
) or the
recognition principle for
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalences
(
2.24
)). It also comes with its own
Yoneda lemma for
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations
(
2.31
). Most importantly, we present a
Grothendieck construction
for
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over an appropriate base (
3.42
), which proves
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration over a nerve
N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{C}
is equivalent to
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
\mathscr{C}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
.

In the last step we need to localize
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations to obtain fibrations which have the appropriately localized values: the
S
-localized
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration (
4.10
). We will show that most results regarding
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations will carry over to its localized version. In particular it again comes with a model structure (
4.17
), with similar properties, such as
invariance
(
4.80
) and
recognition of weak equivalences
(
4.64
). Crucially, we get a
Grothendieck construction
for
S
-localized
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations, which gives us an equivalence between
S
-localized
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over
N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{C}
and
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
\mathscr{C}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
valued in
S
-local objects (
4.51
).

0.4.
Coming back to Fibration of
(\infty,n)
-Categories

Having developed such a general theory of
S
-localized fibrations for
\mathcal{D}
-spaces we can construct fibrations of
(\infty,n)
-categories, which is the content of
Section

5
. The results will apply to various fibrations (
5.16
) and various bases (
5.8
), however, here we will focus on the case of
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibrations over
(\infty,n+1)
-categories, as it is expected that this is the most relevant example. Recall the category of simplicial presheaves on
\Theta_{k}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{n-k}
carries a model structure where the fibrant objects are
(\infty,n)
-categories (
[
BR20
, Corollary 7.2]
, also reviewed in
Section

5.1
).

Theorem.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be
\Theta_{k}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{n-k+1}
-space that is an
(\infty,n+1)
-category, then there is a simplicial left proper combinatorial model structure on the over-category
s\mathscr{P}(\Theta_{k}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{n-k+1})_{/\mathscr{C}}
, called the
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian model structure, that has the following properties.

•

Enrichment:
The
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian model structure is enriched over the model structure for
(\infty,n)
-categories if
k=n
(
5.15
).

•

Fiber-wise Criterion for Fibrations:
A map
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
is an
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibration (meaning it is fibrant in the
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian model structure) if it is an
(\infty,n)
-left fibration (
5.16
) and fiber-wise an
(\infty,n)
-category (
5.20
).

•

Yoneda lemma:
For an object
c
in
\mathscr{C}
, there is a representable
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibration
\mathscr{C}_{c/}\to\mathscr{C}
(
5.25
) and for every
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibration
\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{C}
we have an equivalence of
(\infty,n)
-categories

\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{c}L\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{/\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{C}_{c/},L)

for every object
c
in
\mathscr{C}
(
5.26
).

•

Fiber-wise Dwyer-Kan Equivalences:
A map
\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{L}^{\prime}
of Segal
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibrations over
\mathscr{C}
(
5.16
) is an
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian equivalence if and only if for every object
c
, the map of fibers
\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{c}\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{c}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence of Segal
(\infty,n)
-categories (
5.30
).

•

Recognition Principle for coCartesian Equivalences:

F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{E}
over
\mathscr{C}
is an
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian equivalence if and only if

(\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{/c})^{grpd}\to(\mathscr{E}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{/c})^{grpd}

is an
(\infty,n)
-category equivalence for all objects
c
in
\mathscr{C}
(
5.33
). Here
(-)^{grpd}
is the groupoidification
(\infty,n)
-category (
5.31
).

•

Twisted Arrows:
Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
(\infty,n+1)
-category. There exists an
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibration
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{op}\times\mathscr{C}
, with fiber over
(x,y)
the mapping
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathrm{map}_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)
(
5.35
).

•

Invariance of coCartesian Fibrations:
A Dwyer-Kan equivalence
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
of Segal
(\infty,n+1)
-categories (
5.29
) induces a Quillen equivalence of
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian model structures (
5.40
).

•

Exponentiability:
If
n=k
and
p:\mathscr{R}\to\mathscr{C}
is an
(\infty,n)
-right or
(\infty,n)
-left fibration, then the adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/\mathscr{C}})^{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/\mathscr{R}})^{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a Quillen adjunction (
5.46
), were both sides have the
(\infty,n)
-category model structure.

•

Grothendieck Construction:
For an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
, there are Quillen equivalences (
5.50
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})^{inj_{S}})^{proj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{C}})^{(cov_{(\infty,n)})_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})^{inj_{S}})^{proj}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\hskip-2.8903pt\int_{\mathcal{D}/\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

,

which, if
n=k
, gives us an equivalence of
(\infty,n+1)
-categories (
5.56
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n+1)}}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{art}_{(\infty,n)/\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

.

Under this equivalence the twisted arrow construction (
5.35
) corresponds to the mapping functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-):\mathscr{C}^{op}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n)}
(
5.57
).

•

Universality and Univalence:
There exists a universal
(\infty,n)
-coCartesian fibration (
5.58
)
\pi_{*}:(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n)})_{[0]/}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,n)}
, which is univalent (
5.60
).

0.5.
Applications of
(\infty,n)
-Cartesian Fibrations

What are the applications of a theory of fibrations of
(\infty,n)
-categories? Given the prominent role Cartesian fibrations play in
(\infty,1)
-category theory, we would expect a similar role for
(\infty,n)
-Cartesian fibrations. However, we can also point to several concrete applications that could result from this work:

(1)

Topological Field Theories:

Topological field theories
(TFT’s) are functors out of the
(\infty,n)
-category of
bordisms
and lie at the intersection of
physics

[
Wit88
]
,
higher category theory

[
BD95
]
and
differential geometry

[
Lur09c
,
ST11
]
and have hence attracted the attention from various kinds of mathematicians. Unfortunately, given their higher categorical nature constructing field theories has proven challenging, as they are obtained by constructing
(\infty,n)
-functors out of the
n
-fold Segal spaces of bordism
[
CS19
]
and constructing functors often involves dealing with infinite coherence conditions. Hence many different ideas, such as
2
-Segal sets

[
Tur10
,
DK19
]
,
derived algebraic geometry

[
CHS21
]
,
stable homotopy theory

[
FH21
]
and
homological constructions

[
MMST20
]
have been used to construct and study specific field theories.

As we outlined above, in the world of
(\infty,1)
-categories Cartesian fibrations have been used very effectively to construct many important functors
[
Lur09a
,
Lur17
]
. Combining these observations we would hope to construct topological field theories by constructing appropriate fibrations over the
n
-fold Segal space of bordisms.

(2)

(\infty,n)
-Univalence:
The concept of
univalence
first arose in
homotopy type theory

[
Uni13
]
and made its way into higher category theory in order to prove the existence of models of homotopy type theories in the higher categorical setting
[
KL12
,
GK17
]
. However, we can also study univalence independent of its relation to homotopy type theory via
representability of Cartesian fibrations

[
Ras21c
,
Ras17a
]
.

Given the effectiveness of Cartesian fibrations in the study of univalence, our next expected step is to use
(\infty,n)
-Cartesian fibrations to study
(\infty,n)
-univalence. This would also involve understanding
representable
(\infty,n)
-Cartesian fibrations
. While
(\infty,n)
-univalence could be defined and studied completely in terms of
(\infty,n)
-categories it should still have connections to homotopy type theory. In particular, the
(\infty,2)
-categorical univalence is expected to have connections to the
directed univalence axiom

[
Rie18
]
.

(3)

(\infty,n)
-Topos Theory:
The development of
Grothendieck
(\infty,1)
-toposes
(called
\infty
-topos in
[
Lur09a
]
) was strongly guided by a decent understanding of
Grothendieck
1
-toposes
. In particular, we know that a locally presentable
1
-category is a Grothendieck
1
-topos if it satisfies certain conditions known as the
Giraud axioms

[
MLM94
]
(also called
weak descent
in
[
Rez10b
]
). This has been lifted directly to the
\infty
-categorical setting by Lurie
[
Lur09a
, Section 6]
and Rezk
[
Rez10b
]
. Unfortunately the
n
-categorical situation is far less clear. It is not completely understood which conditions we should impose on a locally presentable
n
-category in order to get a working notion of a Grothendieck
n
-topos and even the
2
-categorical case is an active field of research
[
Web07
]
. Accordingly, there is also no analogous definition for a Grothendieck
(\infty,n)
-topos.

On the other hand, we can now characterize
(\infty,1)
-toposes via the existence of univalent morphisms
[
GK17
,
Ras18b
,
Ste20
]
. Hence, assuming the development of univalent morphisms in the
(\infty,n)
-setting (as outlined in the previous item), we could reasonably expect to define
(\infty,n)
-toposes in a completely analogous manner, without having to worry about the
(\infty,n)
-categorical analogues of presentability and Giraud’s axioms.

0.6.
Outline

The first section (
Section

1
) provides a review and sets relevant notation. In particular, we quickly review enriched model categories via Joyal-Tierney calculus (
Section

1.1
) and enriched categories (
Section

1.2
), however, spend a lot of time and develop a lot of notation for categories enriched over presheaf categories in
Section

1.3
as it is a key concept in this paper. We then focus on two classes of model structures on presheaf categories, level-wise model structures in
Section

1.4
and enriched model structures in
Section

1.5
. Finally, as we want to study fibrations, we also review discrete Grothendieck opfibrations in
Section

1.6
and left fibrations in
Section

1.7
.

We then start our study of
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations in
Section

2
. In the first subsection,
Section

2.1
, we focus on various alternative characterizations and elementary properties. We then move on to prove that
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations come with a model structure and study its properties in
Section

2.2
. Finally, we also review the Yoneda lemma for
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations in
Section

2.3
.

In the next section,
Section

3
, we move on to a key result regarding
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations: the Grothendieck constructions. We proceed in three steps:

(1)

First a strict Grothendieck construction relating diagrams of Grothendieck opfibrations to
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
(
Section

3.1
).

(2)

Then a homotopical Grothendieck construction relating
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over nerves to
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
F:\mathscr{C}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
via model structures (
Section

3.2
).

(3)

Finally, a generalization to
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over very general
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces (
Section

3.3
).

Section

4
localizes most results in
Section

2
and
Section

3
. In particular,
Section

4.1
constructs model structure for localized
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations, also giving some first properties.
Section

4.2
localizes the Grothendieck construction given in
Section

3.2
. Finally,
Section

4.3
uses the Grothendieck construction to study more refined features of the localized
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure.

In
Section

5
we finally apply all results in
Section

4
to the particular case of
(\infty,n)
-fibrations (
Section

5.2
), after a quick review of simplicial presheaf models of
(\infty,n)
-categories in
Section

5.1
. In
Section

5.3
, we focus on some examples and counter-examples.

Finally, in
Section

6
we discuss several possible next steps based on the work in this paper.

0.7.
Background

We will assume familiarity with model category theory as introduced in
[
Hov99
,
DS95
]
and in particular Bousfield localizations
[
Hir03
]
. We will also extensively use results regarding left fibrations of simplicial spaces as introduced in
[
Ras17b
]
either directly or by translating important concepts and there is only a quick review in
Section

1.7
. We will also use ideas from enriched category theory as given in
[
Kel05
]
, however most relevant concepts have been reviewed in
Section

1.2
. For
Section

3.3
we rely on results regarding Segal categories by Bergner
[
Ber07
]
as well enriched quasi-categories by Gepner and Haugseng
[
GH15
]
. Finally,
Section

5
fundamentally relies on the results regarding
(\infty,n)
-categories due to Rezk
[
Rez10a
]
and Bergner and Rezk
[
BR13a
,
BR20
]
.

0.8.
Notation

Throughout we mostly work with categories enriched in the presheaf category
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
and simplicial presheaf category
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
and so use various notation regarding enrichments.

•

If
\mathscr{C}
is enriched over
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
we denote the
set
of maps between them by
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
and the enriched morphisms by
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
. For a given object
X
and maps

g:Y\to X,Z\to X
, we will denote the set of maps
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{/X}}(Y,Z)
by
\mathrm{Hom}_{/X}(Y,Z)
and similarly use
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{/X}(Y,Z)
.

•

There is one exception to the previous rule. If
\mathscr{C}
is a category of functors, then we denote the

set of natural transformations
from
F
to
G
by
\mathrm{Nat}(F,G)
, following conventional notation, and the enriched version by
\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}(F,G)
.

•

If
\mathscr{C}
is enriched over
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
, we denote the
mapping simplicial set
by
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
and the enriched mapping space by
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
. Similar to the last one we will, instead of
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}_{/X}}(Y,Z)
, use
\mathrm{Map}_{/X}(Y,Z)
and
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{/X}(Y,Z)
.

•

If
\mathscr{C}
is Cartesian closed, we denote the internal mapping object by
Y^{X}
.

For a functor between small categories
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
and bicomplete category
\mathscr{E}
we use the following notation for the induced
functors at the level of presheaf categories:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}}

Here
F^{*}
is defined by precomposition,
F_{!}
is the left Kan extension and
F^{*}
the right Kan extension.

Similarly, for a given morphism
f:c\to d
in a category
\mathscr{C}
with small limits and colimits we denote the adjunctions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{/c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{/d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

where
f_{!}
is the post-composition functor,
f^{*}
the pullback functor and
f_{*}
is the right adjoint to
f^{*}
.

Finally, let
\mathscr{C}
be a category with final object
1
. Then we use notation
\{y\}:X\to Y

for the unique map that factors through the map
1\to Y
that picks out the element
y
in
Y
.

0.9.
Acknowledgments

I want to thank Lyne Moser for many interesting conversations about the Grothendieck construction. I would also like to thank the Max-Planck-Institut für Mathematik for its hospitality and financial support.

1.
A Deep Dive into Enrichments and Some Fibrations

In this section we review several relevant concepts that we use throughout.

1.1.
Joyal-Tierney Calculus and Enriched Model Categories

We make extensive use of Cartesian and enriched model categories and so quickly review the relevant notation and concepts motivated by language introduced by Joyal and Tierney
[
JT07
, Section 7]
.

Notation 1.1
.

For this subsection let
\mathscr{C}
be a locally Cartesian closed bicomplete category.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
f:A\to B
and
g:C\to D
be two maps in
\mathscr{C}
. We define the pushout product
as the universal map out of the pushout

f\square g:A\times D\coprod_{A\times C}B\times C\to B\times D

induced by the commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle A\times D\coprod_{A\times C}B\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\times g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\mathrm{id}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times id_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{B}\times g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\square g}

.

Moreover, for two sets of maps
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
we use the notation

\mathcal{A}\square\mathcal{B}=\{f\square g:f\in\mathcal{A},g\in\mathcal{B}\}.

Definition 1.3
.

For two maps
f:A\to B
and
p:Y\to X
we define the pullback exponential

\mathrm{exp}(f,p):Y^{B}\to Y^{A}\underset{X^{A}}{\times}X^{B}

induced by the commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{A}\underset{X^{A}}{\times}X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{exp}(f,p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{f}}

.

Moreover, for two sets of maps
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{X}
we use the notation

\mathrm{exp}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{X})=\{\mathrm{exp}(f,p):f\in\mathcal{A},p\in\mathcal{X}\}

These two functors give us an adjunction of arrow categories:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\square f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{exp}(f,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

.

The key result about these two constructions is that they can help us better understand lifting conditions.

Notation 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
be two sets of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.
If
\mathcal{L}
has the left lifting property with respect to
\mathcal{R}
then we use the notation

\mathcal{L}\pitchfork\mathcal{R}
.

Proposition 1.5
.

(
[
JT07
, Proposition 7.6]
)
Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{X}
be three sets of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
. Then:

\mathcal{A}\square\mathcal{B}\pitchfork\mathcal{X}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{A}\pitchfork\mathrm{exp}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{X})\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{B}\pitchfork\mathrm{exp}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{X}).

We will assume familiarity with standard results of model category theory as given in
[
Hov99
,
Hir03
,
DS95
]
. We will in particular assume familiarity with the theory of Bousfield localization of left proper simplicial combinatorial model categories. However, we will use pushout products and pullback exponentials to quickly review Cartesian and enriched model categories.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}^{\mathcal{M}}
be a model category. We say the model structure
\mathcal{M}
is
Cartesian
if it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions

•

For two cofibrations
i,j

i\square j
is a cofibration, which is trivial if either is trivial.

•

For a cofibration
i
and fibration
p
,
\mathrm{exp}(i,p)
is a fibration, which is trivial if either is trivial.

•

For two cofibrations
i,j

i\square j
is a cofibration. Moreover for each object
A
,
(\emptyset\to A)\square i
is a trivial cofibration if
i
is a trivial cofibration.

•

For two cofibrations
i,j

i\square j
is a cofibrations. Moreover for each object
A
in
\mathscr{C}
the functor
A\times-:\mathscr{C}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\mathcal{M}}
is left Quillen.

Using Cartesian model structures we can define enriched model structures.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
\mathscr{C}^{\mathcal{M}}
be a category
\mathscr{C}
with a Cartesian model structure
\mathcal{M}
. Let
\mathscr{D}^{\mathcal{N}}
be enriched and (co)tensored over
\mathscr{C}
, where the tensor is given by the functor
-\otimes-:\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
. Then we say
\mathscr{D}^{\mathcal{N}}
is enriched over
\mathscr{C}^{\mathcal{M}}
if it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions.

•

For an
\mathcal{M}
-cofibration
i
and an
\mathcal{N}
-cofibration
j
,
i\square j
is an
\mathcal{N}
-cofibration, which is trivial if either is trivial.

•

For an
\mathcal{M}
-cofibration
i
and an
\mathcal{N}
-fibration
p
,
\mathrm{exp}(i,p)
is an
\mathcal{N}
-fibration, which is trivial if either is trivial.

•

For an
\mathcal{M}
-cofibration
i
and an
\mathcal{N}
-cofibration
j
,
i\square j
is an
\mathcal{N}
-cofibration. For each object
A
in
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathcal{N}
-trivial cofibration
j
,
(\emptyset\to A)\square j
is an
\mathcal{N}
-trivial cofibration. For each
B
in
\mathscr{D}
and an
\mathcal{M}
-trivial cofibration
i
,
i\square(\emptyset\to B)
is a
\mathcal{N}
-trivial cofibration.

•

For two cofibrations
i,j

i\square j
is a cofibrations. Moreover for each object
A
in
\mathscr{C}
the functor
A\otimes-:\mathscr{D}^{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathscr{D}^{\mathcal{N}}
is left Quillen and for each object
B
in
\mathscr{D}
the functor
-\otimes B:\mathscr{C}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathscr{D}^{\mathcal{N}}
is left Quillen.

Remark 1.8
.

Notice the fact that the first two conditions are equivalent follows from
1.5
and the fact that the first and third coincide follows from
[
JT07
, Proposition 7.27]
. The equivalence of the third and fourth condition is just the definition of a left Quillen functor.

1.2.
Review of Enriched Categories

In this short section we review some basic aspects of enriched category theory. The main source on enriched category theory is
[
Kel05
]
, but
[
Rie14
, Chapter 3]
is also very readable introduction to this subject. Let
\mathscr{V}
be a category with finite products. Then
(\mathscr{V},\times,1_{\mathscr{V}})
is a symmetric monoidal category, where the symmetric monoidal structure comes from the product and the unit is simply the final object.

Definition 1.9
.

A
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
is a collection of objects
\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}
and for two objects
X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}
an object
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,Y)
in
\mathscr{V}
along with composition maps

\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(Y,Z)\to\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,Z)

and identity elements
1\to\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,X)
, such that composition is unital and associative.

There are alternative ways to characterize the data of an enriched category.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category. We say
\mathscr{C}
is
tensored over
\mathscr{V}
if for all objects
X
in
\mathscr{C}
, the functor
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,-):\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{V}
has a left adjoint
-\otimes_{\mathscr{V}}X:\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{C}
.

In general an enriched category is not necessarily tensored, however, there are important exceptions.

Proposition 1.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category so that the underlying category is locally presentable. Then
\mathscr{C}
is tensored over
\mathscr{V}
and the tensor
-\otimes_{\mathscr{V}}-:\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{C}
uniquely determines the enrichment.

We will make use of the functor category of enriched categories and so will review it here as well.

Definition 1.12
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be two
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories. A
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functor
of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is a map of objects
F_{\mathrm{Obj}}:\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{Obj}}\to\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Obj}}
and for any two objects
X,Y
in
\mathscr{C}
a morphism in
\mathscr{V}

F(X,Y):\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,Y)\to\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{V}}(FX,FY)

such that
F
respects composition and the identity.

Notation 1.13
.

We will denote the category with objects
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories and morphisms
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functors by
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{V}}
.

Having functors we can define natural transformations.

Definition 1.14
.

Let
F,G:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be two
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functors of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories. A
\mathscr{V}
-enriched natural transformation
of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functors is a collection of morphisms
\alpha_{X}:1\to\mathscr{D}(FX,GX)
such that for all objects
X,Y
the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{V}}(FX,FY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{V}}(GX,GY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{V}}(FX,GY)}

.

We denote the
set
of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched natural transformations by
\mathrm{Nat}(F,G)
.

Definition 1.15
.

Let
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}
be two
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories. Define the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
as the category (not enriched) with objects
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functors
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
and morphisms
\mathscr{V}
-enriched natural transformations
\mathrm{Nat}(F,G)
.

We can use tensors to add an enrichment to
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
.

Definition 1.16
.

Let
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}
be two
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories, such that
\mathscr{D}
is tensored and cotensored over
\mathscr{V}
. Then the category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
tensored and cotensored over
\mathscr{V}
defined point-wise as
(F\otimes_{\mathscr{V}}X)(C)=F(C)\otimes_{\mathscr{V}}X
. In particular, if
\mathscr{C}
is small and the underlying category
\mathscr{D}
is locally presentable, then
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
is
\mathscr{V}
-enriched. We denote the
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category by
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
and the
\mathscr{V}
-enriched natural transformations
\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}(F,G)
.

For a proof of the point-wise tensor and cotensor see
[
Mos19
, Lemma 5.2]
. One key aspect of enrichments are change of enrichment functors. Let
(\mathscr{W},\times,1_{\mathscr{W}})
be another category with finite products and let
\mathrm{Fun}^{\times}(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{W})
denote the category of product preserving functors from
\mathscr{V}
to
\mathscr{W}
. Then we have the following result.

Lemma 1.17
.

There is a change-of-enrichment functor

\mathscr{C}\mathrm{h}:\mathrm{Fun}^{\times}(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{W})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{V}},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{W}})

defined as follows. For a given functor
F:\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{W}
define
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{h}(F):\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{V}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{W}}
that takes a
\mathscr{V}
-category
\mathscr{C}
to the
\mathscr{W}
-category
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{h}(F)(\mathscr{C})
which has the same objects and
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{h}(F)(\mathscr{C})_{\mathscr{W}}(X,Y)=F(\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(X,Y))
.

For a proof see
[
EK66
, Proposition 6.3, Theorem 6.4]
. Let us give the key example of an enriched category.

Definition 1.18
.

Let
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
be the category of small sets and set functions. Then a
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
-enriched category is precisely a category, which we will also call strict
1
-category. By induction a
strict
n
-category
is defined as a
(n-1)
-enriched category. We denote the category of strict
n
-categories and enriched functors by
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{n}
. For two strict
n
-categories we denote the
(n-1)
-category of functors by
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
.

We use Cartesian closure to construct many interesting enriched categories, as explained in
[
Rie14
, Definition 3.3.6]
.

Lemma 1.19
.

Let
\mathscr{V}
be a Cartesian closed category. Then
\mathscr{V}
is enriched over itself with
\mathscr{C}(X,Y)=Y^{X}
.

We shall use this lemma to give further examples of enriched categories in the next section.

Remark 1.20
.

The enrichment we consider here throughout is a
strict enrichment
. For example,
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
-enriched categories are strict
2
-categories and not bicategories (where associativity and unitality holds only up to natural isomorphism). There are a few occasions (
3.61
,
4.57
,
5.52
) where we consider weakly enriched quasi-categories in sense of
[
GH15
]
.

1.3.
Categories Enriched over Categories of Presheaves

In this section want to review categories enriched over categories of set-valued presheaves. First we review the desired diagram categories.
Let
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
be the category of finite linearly ordered sets with linearly ordered functions. We denote the objects by
[0],[1],[2],\cdots
.

Notation 1.21
.

Every morphism
[n]\to[m]
is uniquely characterized by an increasing sequence of
0\leq k_{1}\leq...\leq k_{n}\leq m
. We denote this morphism by
<k_{1},...,k_{n}>:[n]\to[m]
. In particular every morphism
[0]\to[n]
is of the form
<i>
, where
0\leq i\leq n
.

For more details regarding the category
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
see
[
GJ99
]
or the initial section of
[
Rez01
]
or
[
Ras17b
]
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category. Let
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
, which we call the category of
\mathcal{D}
-sets. Moreover, let
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
, which we call the category of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets.

We denote the
representable
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets
by
F[d,n]
, where
d
is an object in
\mathcal{D}
and an
[n]
is an object in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
. We can use the representable functors to deduce that the category of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets is
Cartesian closed
, where for two
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets
X,Y
the Cartesian closure
Y^{X}
is uniquely characterized by the natural bijection

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(F[d,n],Y^{X})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(F[d,n]\times X,Y)

Using the Cartesian closure and
1.19
we get the following result.

Lemma 1.22
.

The category of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets is enriched over itself. For two
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets
X,Y
we denote the enriched morphisms by
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(X,Y)
and note it is defined as

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(X,Y)[d,n]=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(F[d,n]\times X,Y)

Following
1.13
we use
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
(
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
) to denote the category of
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched (
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
-enriched) categories. Let us see the key example of a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category.

Example 1.23
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category. Then, by
1.16
, the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))
is
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched with tensor given by
(F\times X)(c)=F(c)\times X
, where
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
is a functor and
X
a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial set. In particular, for two functors
F,G:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
, the
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial set
\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}(F,G)
is defined as

\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}(F,G)[d]=\mathrm{Nat}(F\times\{F[d]\},G),

where
\{F[d]\}
denotes the constant functor with value
F[d]
. We will denote
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor category by
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))
following
1.16
.

Given two enriched categories
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{E}
we want an easy criterion that can help us determine when a functor
G:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{E}
is in fact a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor.

Lemma 1.24
.

Let
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{E}
be two
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories such that they are tensored over
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
(
1.10
). Moreover, let
G:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{E}
be a functor of underlying categories such that
G(X\otimes F[d])=G(X)\otimes F[d]
. Then
G
is a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor.

Proof.

Notice, for two objects
X,Y
,
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)[d]=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X\otimes F[d],Y)
and so we can define
G:\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X,Y)\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(GX,GY)
level-wise as

G[d]:\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)[d]=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X\otimes F[d],Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(GX\otimes F[d],GY)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{E}}(GX,GY)[d],

where we are using the fact that there is a functor
\mathrm{Hom}(-\otimes-,-):\mathcal{D}^{op}\times\mathscr{C}^{op}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
.
∎

Some enriched functors need to be defined using alternative methods.

Lemma 1.25
.

The product functor
-\times-:\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\times\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
is
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched.

Proof.

Fix four objects
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
and let
\mathrm{ev}_{X,X^{\prime}}:X\times\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X,X^{\prime})\to X^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{Y,Y^{\prime}}:Y\times\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(Y,Y^{\prime})\to Y^{\prime}
be the evaluation functors. We define

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(X,X^{\prime})\times\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(Y,Y^{\prime})\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(X\times Y,X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime})

as the adjoint of the product map
\mathrm{ev}_{X,X^{\prime}}\times\mathrm{ev}_{Y,Y^{\prime}}
.
∎

We have a similar result for adjunctions.

Lemma 1.26
.

Let
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{E}
be two
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories such that they are tensored over
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
(
1.10
). Moreover, let
L:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{E}
and
R:\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{C}
two
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors such that
L\dashv R
is an adjunction of underlying categories. If
L(X\otimes F[d])=LX\otimes F[d]
, then
L\dashv R
is an enriched adjunction.

Proof.

For every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
we have a natural isomorphism

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{E}}(LX,Y)[d]=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(LX\otimes F[d],Y)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(L(X\otimes F[d]),Y)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X\otimes F[d],RY)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,RY)[d].

∎

Notice, there is an enriched Yoneda lemma for
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories
[
Kel05
, 2.30]
.

Lemma 1.27
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category. Then for any object
c
in
\mathscr{C}
and enriched functor
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
there an isomorphism of
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-sets
F(c)\cong\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}(\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(c,-),F)
. In particular for any two objects
c,c^{\prime}
there is an isomorphism of
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-sets

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(c^{\prime},c)\cong\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))}(\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(c,-),\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(c^{\prime},-))
.

The enriched Yoneda lemma implies that we have an
enriched Yoneda embedding
as stated in
[
Kel05
, 2.34]
.

Lemma 1.28
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category. There is a fully faithful
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor

\mathrm{Yon}:\mathscr{C}\to\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})),

which takes an object
c
to the representable functor
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,c)
.

Remark 1.29
.

The enriched Yoneda lemma in particular implies that for a given small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
we have an
enriched mapping object
functor

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(-,-):\mathscr{C}^{op}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})

This is
[
Kel05
, Theorem 3.73]
.

Theorem 1.30
.

A
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
is
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-cocomplete if the underlying category has all small colimits and it is tensored over
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
. Moreover, a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
between
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-cocomplete categories preserves
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-colimits if the underlying functor preserves colimits and preserves the tensor.

We can use the enriched Yoneda lemma to study enriched Kan extensions
[
Kel05
, Theorem 4.51]
.

Theorem 1.31
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category and
\mathscr{D}
a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category with
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-colimits. Then there is an isomorphism between
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
and colimit preserving
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))\to\mathscr{D}
, where the equivalence is given via left Kan extension and restriction along the Yoneda embedding.

We also want a method to construct enriched functors in
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))
. For that we have the following result
[
Kel05
, 2.20]
.

Theorem 1.32
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category and
\mathscr{D}
a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category. There is an isomorphism of (set-enriched) functor categories

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{op}\times\mathscr{D},s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))\cong\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D},\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C}^{op},s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})))

We can generalize the
nerve
to a
\mathcal{D}
-nerve
.

Definition 1.33
.

Let

N_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})

be the functor which takes an
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
to the
\mathscr{D}
-simplicial set

N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{C}[k]=\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{k}}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X_{0},X_{1})\times...\times\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X_{k-1},X_{k})

where the boundary maps are degeneracy maps are given by composition and inserting identity maps.

The nerve construction respects opposites. Let

(1.34)

(-)^{op}:\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})

be the functor that precomposes a functor
F:\mathcal{D}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
with
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}^{op}}\times\sigma^{op}
, where
\sigma:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
is unique automorphism. We now have the following simple result.

Lemma 1.35
.

The following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathcal{D}}}

where the left hand
(-)^{op}
takes a category to the opposite category.

Notice the nerve behaves the way we would expect. First we recall the Segal condition.

Definition 1.36
.

A simplicial set
X:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
satisfies the
strict Segal condition
if for all
n\geq 2
the evident map

X_{n}\to X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}...\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}

is an isomorphism of sets.

Proposition 1.37
.

The
\mathcal{D}
-nerve is fully faithful with essential image given by
F:\mathcal{D}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
such that for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, the simplicial set
F[d,-]:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
satisfies the strict Segal condition and the functor
F[-,0]:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
is a constant functor.

We need several functors regarding
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets that we will now introduce.

Definition 1.38
.

Let
d
be an element in
\mathcal{D}
. Then this gives us a functor
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
that gives us an adjunction on presheaf categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

We call the right adjoint
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}
the
underlying simplicial set
and we have in particular,
\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{d}(F[n])=F[d,n]
.

Definition 1.39
.

We have a projection functor
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
which gives us a functor
\mathscr{D}\mathrm{isc}:s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
, which we call the
discrete inclusion
.

Remark 1.40
.

We use the notation
D[n]=\mathscr{D}\mathrm{isc}(F[n])
and notice that
D[n]\times F[d,0]\cong F[d,n]
. In particular if
\mathcal{D}
has a terminal object
t
, then
D[n]\cong F[t,n]
.

Definition 1.41
.

Fix
k
in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
, then we have an inclusion map
\mathrm{incl}_{k}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
which induces an adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{incl}_{k})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{incl}_{k})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

We denote the right adjoint
(\mathrm{incl}_{k})^{*}
also by
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}_{k}:\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
and call it the
k
-th value functor
. If
k=0
then we will simply denote it by
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}:\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
and call it the
value
. Moreover, we denote the left adjoint
(\mathrm{incl}_{0})_{!}
by
\mathscr{V}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{mb}
and call it the
value embedding
.

Up until now we have focused on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets, however, as our goal is to do homotopy theory we need analogous definitions for
\mathcal{D}
-diagrams valued in spaces.

Definition 1.42
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be the category of simplicial sets which we call
spaces
. The representable spaces are denoted
\Delta[l]
.

Remark 1.43
.

Notice there is an apparent overlap, namely if we take
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
with
\mathcal{D}
the trivial category then it coincides with
1.42
. However, the key is to consider
\mathscr{S}
as a homotopical category with a model structure, which is why we call the objects in
\mathscr{S}
spaces. In particular, it should be replaceable with any appropriately Quillen equivalent model structure, without changing the results, whereas
s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}=\mathscr{P}({\mathbbe{\Delta}})
is treated as a category without any model structure.

We can now repeat all the definitions we have given up until now replacing sets with spaces.

Remark 1.44
.

We have following facts regarding simplicial spaces:

(1)

A
\mathcal{D}
-space is a functor
X:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}
and the category of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces is denoted by
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
.

(2)

A
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space is a functor
X:\mathcal{D}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}
and the category of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces is denoted by
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
.

(3)

We denote the representable
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces by
F[d,k]\times\Delta[l]
.

(4)

We can use representable simplicial spaces to observe that
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
is Cartesian closed with

\mathrm{Hom}_{s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(F[d,k]\times\Delta[l],Y^{X})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(F[d,k]\times\Delta[l]\times X,Y)

which in particular means
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
is enriched over itself (
1.19
). We denote the enrichment by
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}(X,Y)
and category of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories by
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
.

(5)

Following
1.23
for a a given small
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
, we can define the
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor category
\underline{\mathrm{Fun}}(\mathscr{C},s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))
, which is point-wise tensored and cotensored over
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
, hence for two
s\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{D})
-enriched functors
F,G
we have

\underline{\mathrm{Nat}}(F,G)[d,k]=\mathrm{Nat}(F\times\{F[d,k]\},G),

where
\{F[d,k]\}:\mathscr{C}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
is the constant functor.

(6)

We can define a functor

N_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})

similar to
1.33
and notice similar to
1.35
it commutes with the opposite category construction.

(7)

Following
1.38
for every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
we define an adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

where the right adjoint
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}
is called the
underlying simplicial space
.

(8)

Similar to
1.39
we define the functor
\mathscr{D}\mathrm{isc}:s\mathscr{S}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
.

(9)

Analogous to
1.41
we define an adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{incl}_{k})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{incl}_{k})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

and use the notation
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}_{k}:s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
and in particular
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}=\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}_{0}:s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
for the right adjoint, which we call the
value
. Moreover, we denote the left adjoint by
\mathscr{V}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{mb}:s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
, which we call the value embedding.

In addition to the previous constructions we also have diagonal functors.

Definition 1.45
.

Let
\mathrm{Diag}:\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
given by
\mathrm{Diag}(d,k)=(d,k,k)
. Then we get an adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

To simplify notation we use
\mathscr{D}\mathrm{iag}=\mathrm{Diag}^{*}:s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
for the left adjoint.

We can also use
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces to construct model structures.

Proposition 1.46
.

There is a unique simplicial proper combinatorial Cartesian model structure on the category of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
(
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
), called the injective model structure and denoted
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})^{inj}
(
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{inj}
), with the following specifications:

W

weak equivalences level-wise Kan equivalence of spaces

C

Cofibrations are level-wise injections.

F

Fibrations are morphisms that have the right lifting property with respect to trivial cofibrations.

For a proof see
[
Hel82
,
Hel83
]
. We can use the injective model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-spaces to define
Dwyer-Kan equivalences of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories
.

Definition 1.47
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category with terminal object
t
.
Let
G:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories. We say
G
is a
Dwyer-Kan equivalence
if the following two conditions hold.

•

For any two objects
c,c^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}
, the induced map of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(c,c^{\prime})\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}(Fc,Fc^{\prime})

is an injective equivalence.

•

The homotopy category of the underlying functor of simplicially enriched categories
\mathrm{Ho}G[t]:\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{C}[t]\to\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{D}[t]
is essentially surjective.

1.4.
The Level-Wise Model Structure on
\mathcal{D}
-Simplicial Spaces

In this subsection we introduce a method that allows us to generalize localization model structures from the Reedy model structure on simplicial spaces to a model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. For that we need the following alternative perspective on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces.

Definition 1.48
.

Define the
level-wise Reedy
model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},s\mathscr{S}^{Ree})^{inj}
as the injective model structure on the Reedy model structure on simplicial spaces. More concretely, we can describe the cofibrations and weak equivalences as follows:

•

A map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
f:X\to Y
is a cofibration if for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is a cofibration in the Reedy model structure on simplicial spaces.

•

A map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is a weak equivalence in the Reedy model structure on simplicial spaces.

The characterization has the following immediate result.

Lemma 1.49
.

The level-wise Reedy model structure coincides with the injective model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces.

Proof.

Both model structures have the same weak equivalences (level-wise Kan equivalences) and cofibrations (level wise inclusions).
∎

We can use the level-wise Reedy model structure to easily construct interesting new model structures on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
.

Proposition 1.50
.

Let
S
be a set of cofibrations of simplicial spaces. Then there exist a unique simplicial left proper combinatorial model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces, which we call the level-wise
S
-localized model structure and denote
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{lev_{S}}
, and has the following specifications.

(1)

Cofibrations are inclusions.

(2)

An object
X
is fibrant if it is injectively fibrant and for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is
S
-local.

(3)

A morphism
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if and only if for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is an
S
-local equivalence.

(4)

A weak equivalence (fibration) between fibrant objects is an injective equivalence (injective fibration).

(5)

If the
S
-localized Reedy model structure on simplicial spaces is Cartesian then this model structure is also Cartesian.

Proof.

Let
s\mathscr{S}^{Ree_{S}}
be the
S
-localized Reedy model structure on the category of simplicial spaces. We define the level-wise
S
-localized model structure as the injective model structure on
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},s\mathscr{S}^{Ree_{S}})
and notice it satisfies the conditions given in the statement by definition of the injective model structure.
∎

We now want to use this result to construct several important model structure on the category of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces based on model structures on simplicial spaces. First of all we need two model structures that relate
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces and simplicial spaces, similar to the result in
[
Ras17b
, Subsection 2.5]
.

Theorem 1.51
.

There is a unique, combinatorial, simplicial model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
,
called the
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal model structure and denoted by
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{\mathcal{D}-diag}
, with the following specifications.

(W)

A map
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if the diagonal map

\mathscr{D}\mathrm{iag}(f):\mathscr{D}\mathrm{iag}(X)\to\mathscr{D}\mathrm{iag}(Y)

is an injective equivalence of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces.

(C)

A map
f:X\to Y
is a cofibration if it is an inclusion.

(F)

A map
f:X\to Y
is a fibration if it satisfies the right lifting condition for trivial cofibrations.

In particular, an object
W
is fibrant if and only if it is injectively fibrant and the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(W)
is homotopically constant for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, which is equivalent to the counit map
\mathscr{V}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{mb}\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(W)\to W
be an injective equivalence of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces.

Proof.

Let
s\mathscr{S}^{diag}
be the diagonal model structure on simplicial spaces, which, by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 2.11]
, is obtained by localizing with respect to the cofibrations
F[0]\to F[n]
for all
n\geq 0
. Now applying
1.50
gives us the
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal model structure.
∎

Theorem 1.52
.

There is a unique, cofibrantly generated, simplicial model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
,
called the value model structure and denoted by
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{val}
, with the following specification.

(W)

A map
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if

\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}f:\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}X\to\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}Y

is an injective equivalence of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces.

(C)

A map
f:X\to Y
is a cofibration if it is an inclusion.

(F)

A map
f:X\to Y
is a fibration if it satisfies the right lifting condition for trivial cofibrations.

Proof.

Let
s\mathscr{S}^{Kan}
be the Kan model structure on simplicial spaces, which, by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 2.12]
, is obtained by localizing with respect to the cofibrations
\partial F[n]\to F[n]
for all
n>0
. Now applying
1.50
gives us the value model structure.
∎

We can now relate these model structures to the injective model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-spaces.

Theorem 1.53
.

The
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched adjunctions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{\mathcal{D}-diag}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})^{inj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{val}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}\mathrm{iag}=\mathrm{Diag}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{mb}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}}

are Quillen equivalences.

Proof.

By
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 2.13]
we have simplicial Quillen equivalences
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{S}^{diag}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{Kan}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{S}^{Kan}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1})_{*}}

The result now follows from applying
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},-)
to this adjunction and giving all sides the injective model structure combined with
[
Lur09a
, Remark A.2.8.6]
.
∎

We now use
1.50
to construct model structures on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces based on model structures on simplicial spaces motivated by
(\infty,1)
-category theory.

Definition 1.54
.

A simplicial space
X
is called a
Segal space
if it is Reedy fibrant and for all
n\geq 2
the map

X_{n}\to X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}...\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}

is a Kan equivalence of spaces.

For more details on Segal spaces see the original source
[
Rez01
, Section 5]
.
We can generalize this definition to
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces.

Definition 1.55
.

An
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space
X
is a
level-wise Segal space
if it is injectively fibrant and for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a Segal space.

Segal spaces are the fibrant objects in a model structure on simplicial spaces (
[
Rez01
, Theorem 7.1]
), that is obtained by localizing the Reedy model structure on simplicial spaces with respect to the maps
G[n]\to F[n]
, for
n\geq 2

[
Rez01
, Section 5]
. We can use this observation to show level-wise Segal spaces come with a model structure.

Proposition 1.56
.

There exist a unique simplicial left proper combinatorial Cartesian model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
, which we call the level-wise Segal model structure and denote
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{lev_{Seg}}
, which has the following specifications.

(1)

Cofibrations are inclusions.

(2)

An object
X
is fibrant if it is injectively fibrant and for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a Segal space.

(3)

A morphism
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if and only if for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is a Segal equivalence.

(4)

A weak equivalence (fibration) between fibrant objects is an injective equivalence (injective fibration).

Proof.

This is a direct application of
1.50
with respect to the maps
G[n]\to F[n]
, where
n\geq 2
. Notice the Segal space model structure is in fact Cartesian
[
Rez01
, Theorem 7.1]
, which implies the level-wise Segal space model structure is Cartesian as well.
∎

Segal spaces do not give us a model of
(\infty,1)
-categories, for that we need another condition, called the
completeness condition
.
There two ways to add a condition to a Segal space so that the resulting object is in fact an
(\infty,1)
-category and we will need both of them. The first one is the completeness condition.

Definition 1.57
.

A Segal space
X
is called a
complete Segal space
if the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times X_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

is a homotopy pullback square of spaces.

For more details on complete Segal spaces see
[
Rez01
, Section 6]
. Again complete Segal spaces can be generalized to
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces.

Definition 1.58
.

An
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space
X
is a
level-wise complete Segal space
if it is injectively fibrant and for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a complete Segal space.

Similar to Segal spaces, complete Segal spaces are the fibrant objects in a model structure on simplicial spaces (
[
Rez01
, Theorem 7.2]
), which is obtained by localizing with respect to the cofibrations
G[n]\to F[n]
, where
n\geq 2
, and
F[0]\to E[1]

[
Rez01
, Section 5]
.
Using this we can easily construct a model structure for level-wise complete Segal spaces.

Proposition 1.59
.

There exist a unique simplicial left proper combinatorial Cartesian model structure on
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
, which we call the level-wise complete Segal model structure and denote
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{lev_{CSS}}
, which has the following specifications.

(1)

Cofibrations are inclusions.

(2)

An object
X
is fibrant if it is injectively fibrant and for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a complete Segal space.

(3)

A morphism
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if and only if for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is a complete Segal equivalence.

(4)

A weak equivalence (fibration) between fibrant objects is an injective equivalence (injective fibration).

Proof.

This is a direct application of
1.50
with respect to the maps
G[n]\to F[n]
, where
n\geq 2
, and
F[0]\to E[1]
. Notice the complete Segal space model structure is in fact Cartesian
[
Rez01
, Theorem 7.2]
, which implies the level-wise complete Segal space model structure is Cartesian as well.
∎

In
[
Rez01
, Theorem 7.7]
Rezk proves that a map of Segal spaces is a complete Segal space equivalence if and only if it is fully faithful and essentially surjective, also known as a
Dwyer-Kan equivalence
of Segal spaces (
[
Rez01
, 7.4]
). Using
1.59
we can immediately generalize this to a level-wise version.

Lemma 1.60
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of level-wise Segal spaces. Then
f:X\to Y
is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence if and only if for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence.

The second way is by adding a discreteness condition to Segal spaces.

Definition 1.61
.

A
Segal category

W
is a Segal space such that the space
W_{0}
is discrete.

Segal categories also come with a model structure
[
Ber07
, Theorem 5.1]
, that we can generalize here to
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces

Proposition 1.62
.

There exist a unique simplicial left proper combinatorial model structure on
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
, which we call the level-wise Segal category model structure and denote
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{lev_{SegCat}}
, which has the following specifications.

(1)

Cofibrations are inclusions.

(2)

An object
X
is fibrant if it is injectively fibrant and for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a Segal category.

(3)

A morphism
f:X\to Y
is a weak equivalence if and only if for every
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
is a complete Segal equivalence.

(4)

A weak equivalence (fibration) between fibrant objects is an injective equivalence (injective fibration).

Proof.

This is a direct application of
1.50
to the generating trivial cofibrations in the model structure for Segal categories
[
Ber07
, Theorem 5.1]
.
∎

There is a Quillen equivalence between the model structure for Segal categories and complete Segal space, which we can generalize to an equivalence of level-wise model structure.

Proposition 1.63
.

Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{lev_{SegCat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{lev_{CSS}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

be the adjunction
(I,R)
given in
[
Ber07
, Theorem 6.3]
level-wise. Then this adjunction is a Quillen equivalence.

Proof.

This again follows from
[
Lur09a
, Remark A.2.8.6]
applied to the Quillen equivalence
(I,R)
(
[
Ber07
, Theorem 6.3]
).
∎

Notice,
I_{\mathcal{D}}
is the identity on Segal categories and we will often omit it to simplify notation. The result has the following important implication.

Corollary 1.64
.

Let
X
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space. Then there exists a natural zigzag

X\to\hat{X}\leftarrow R_{\mathcal{D}}\hat{X}

where
\hat{X}
is the fibrant replacement in the level-wise complete Segal space model structure and
R_{\mathcal{D}}\hat{X}
is the corresponding level-wise Segal category and both maps are level-wise complete Segal space equivalences.

1.5.
Enriched (complete) Segal Spaces and Categories

Level-wise (complete) Segal spaces are an important first step towards studying
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category theory via simplicial objects, however, we need to add another condition that gives us a well-defined notion of objects.

Definition 1.65
.

A map of simplicial spaces
X\to Y
is
