essentially surjective
if the map
\hat{X}\to\hat{Y}
is essentially surjective (meaning
\hat{X}_{0}\to\hat{Y}_{0}
is a surjection on path-components). Here
\hat{X}
and
\hat{Y}
are fibrant replacements in the complete Segal space model structure.

Notice this definition does not depend on the choice of fibrant replacement as any two fibrant replacements are Reedy equivalent.

Definition 1.66
.

A
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space
X
has
weakly constant objects
if for for all morphisms
f:d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
the induced map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d^{\prime}}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is essentially surjective.

Notice this condition can be simplified.

Lemma 1.67
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
. Then a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space
X
has weakly constant objects if and only if the map
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is essentially surjective for all objects
d
. Here the morphism is given by the unique morphism
d\to t
.

Definition 1.68
.

An
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space

X
is a level-wise Segal space with weakly constant objects. If
\mathcal{D}
has a terminal object
t
, we denote
X[t,0]=\mathrm{Obj}_{X}
and call it the
objects of
X
.

Similar to Segal spaces this notion has appropriate categorical properties.

Definition 1.69
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
.
Let
x,y
be two objects in the
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space
X
. Define the
mapping
\mathcal{D}
-space

\mathrm{map}_{X}(x,y)
by the pull back of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{map}_{X}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}_{1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(X)\times\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)}

We can use that to define a homotopy category, similar to Segal spaces.

Definition 1.70
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
and let
X
be an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space. Define the
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathrm{Ho}X
, called the
homotopy category
as follows. Objects are given by
(\mathrm{Obj}_{X})_{0}
and for two objects
x,y
define
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ho}X}(x,y)=\pi_{0}(\mathrm{map}_{X}(x,y))
.

Ideally we would like to say that if
X
is an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space then
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(X)
is homotopically constant. We want to show that this statement holds, up to level-wise Dwyer-Kan equivalence. In order to make this into a precise argument we need
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal categories.

Definition 1.71
.

An
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal category

X
is a level-wise Segal category with weakly constant objects. If
\mathcal{D}
has a terminal object
t
, we denote
X[t,0]=\mathrm{Obj}_{X}
and call it the
objects of
X
.

We now want to prove that every
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space is level-wise Dwyer-Kan equivalent to an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal category with constant objects. For that we need codiscrete simplicial objects.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category with finite products. Then the restriction functor
(-)_{0}:\mathrm{Fun}({\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op},\mathscr{C})\to\mathscr{C}
has a right adjoint
\mathrm{co}\mathcal{D}\mathrm{isc}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Fun}({\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op},\mathscr{C})
, which takes an object
X
in
\mathscr{C}
to the simplicial object
\mathrm{co}\mathcal{D}\mathrm{isc}(X)
with
\mathrm{co}\mathcal{D}\mathrm{isc}(X)_{n}=X^{n+1}
and with face and degeneracy maps given by projections and diagonals. We have the following simple result regarding
\mathrm{co}\mathcal{D}\mathrm{isc}
.

Lemma 1.72
.

The simplicially enriched adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{S}^{Seg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{Kan}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{co}\mathcal{D}\mathrm{isc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a Quillen adjunction such that the right adjoint reflects weak equivalences.

Proof.

The left adjoint preserves cofibrations and the right adjoint takes a Kan complex
K
to a Segal space
\mathrm{co}\mathcal{D}\mathrm{isc}(K)
. Hence the adjunction is a Quillen adjunction by
[
Lur09a
, Corollary A.3.7.2]
. In order to prove the right adjoint reflects weak equivalences it suffices to prove that for every space
K
the counit map is an equivalence. However, the counit is just the identity map and hence we are done.
∎

We now have the following important construction. Let
\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{t}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X\to X
be the counit of the adjunction (
1.38
). We can factor this map into a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration

(1.73)

\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{t}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X\to\bar{X}\to X

in the injective model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. Notice, by the triangle identity for adjunction
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{t}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X
is the identity map and so
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}\bar{X}=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X
.

Definition 1.74
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category with a terminal object
t
.
For a given level-wise Segal category
X
define
\mathbb{H}X
as the pullback

(1.75)

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
\bar{X}
is defined via the factorization
1.73
.

Remark 1.76
.

The definition of
\mathbb{H}
is a generalization of the construction of the functor
R
by Bergner in
[
Ber07
, Section 6]
, which is used to construct Segal categories out of complete Segal spaces.

By
1.72
all three parts of the pullback diagram are level-wise Segal spaces and so the
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space
\mathbb{H}X

is also a level-wise Segal category with
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(RX)=X[t,0]
. We now have the following result.

Lemma 1.77
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
. Let
X
be an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal category. The map
\mathbb{H}X\to X
defined in
1.75
is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence if and only if
X
has weakly constant objects.

Proof.

We need to show
RX\to X
is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence. For an object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}RX\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is by definition fully faithful and the inclusion of object
X[t,0]=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}RX[0]\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X[0]
is essentially surjective if and only if
X
has weakly constant objects and so the result follows.
∎

We can combine this result with
1.64
to get the following corollary.

Corollary 1.78
.

Let
X
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space. There exists a natural zigzag

X\to\hat{X}\leftarrow\tilde{X}

which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

\hat{X}
is a level-wise complete Segal space.

(2)

\tilde{X}
is a level-wise Segal category and
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(\tilde{X})
is discrete.

(3)

The first arrow is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence.

(4)

The second map is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence and if and only if
X
has weakly constant objects.

Proof.

Let
\hat{X}
be the fibrant replacement of
X
in the level-wise complete Segal space model structure
1.59
. Define
\tilde{X}=\mathbb{H}R_{\mathcal{D}}\hat{X}
, which is a level-wise Segal category with discrete objects by
1.74
. The first map is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence by definition and the second map is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence if and only if
X
has weakly constant objects by
1.77
.
∎

As a first application we can use this result to better understand equivalences of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal spaces and adjust
1.60
to
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal spaces.

Definition 1.79
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
. A map of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal spaces
f:X\to Y
is an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Dwyer-Kan equivalence
if the following two conditions hold:

•

Fully faithful:
For every two objects
x,y
, the map of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces

\mathrm{map}_{X}(x,y)\to\mathrm{map}_{Y}(fx,fy)

is an injective equivalence of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces.

•

Essentially surjective:
The map of Segal spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}f\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}Y
is essentially surjective.

This new notion relates appropriately with other notions of equivalences.

Lemma 1.80
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
and let
f:X\to Y
be a map of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal spaces. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

f
is a level-wise complete Segal space equivalence.

(2)

f
is a level-wise Dwyer-Kan equivalence.

(3)

f
is an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Dwyer-Kan equivalence.

Proof.

The first two are equivalent by
1.60
. Now, following
1.78
,
f
is a level-wise Dwyer-Kan equivalence if and only if
\tilde{f}
is a level-wise Dwyer-Kan equivalence. However,
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(\tilde{X})
is discrete and so this coincides with being a
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Dwyer-Kan equivalence.
∎

We can in particular apply this to nerves.

Example 1.81
.

Let
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories. Then, by
1.80
,
F
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories (
1.47
) if and only if
N_{\mathcal{D}}F:N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{C}\to N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{D}
is a level-wise (or, equivalently,
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched) Dwyer-Kan equivalence.

The notion of
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space is deliberately chosen to be as general as possible to include many examples of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. The drawback is that it cannot be a fibrant object in a localization model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
. However, this will be relevant in later parts (
Section

5
).

Definition 1.82
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
. An
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched complete Segal space

X
is an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space, such that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{t}X
is complete and
\mathscr{V}\mathrm{al}(X)
is homotopically constant.

Theorem 1.83
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category with terminal object
t
.
There exists a simplicial combinatorial left proper model structure on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces, called the
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched complete Segal space model structure and denoted by
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})^{\mathcal{D}-CSS}
, which has the following properties:

•

The cofibrations are monomorphisms.

•

An object
W
is fibrant if it is an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched complete Segal space.

•

A morphism
f:A\to B
is a weak equivalence if and only if for every
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched complete Segal space the map

f^{*}:\mathrm{Map}(B,W)\to\mathrm{Map}(A,W)

is a Kan equivalence.

•

Fibrations (weak equivalences) between fibrant objects are injective fibrations (weak equivalences).

•

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space with homotopically constant value, then the fibrant replacement
W\to\hat{W}
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence (
1.47
).

Proof.

The model structure is a left Bousfield localization with respect to the following sets of morphisms:

•

\mathscr{D}\mathrm{isc}(G[n])\times F[d]\to F[d,n]

•

D[0]\to\mathscr{D}\mathrm{isc}(E[1])

•

F[t,0]\to F[d,0]

By definition the fibrant objects are the
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched complete Segal spaces. The other properties follow formally from Bousfield localizations.

The last part follows from the same steps as the proof in
[
BR20
, Lemma 8.19]
. Notice the proof there is given for a specific diagram category, rather than a general
\mathcal{D}
, however, it only uses the fact that the diagram has a terminal object.
∎

Remark 1.84
.

Notice, every
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space is level-wise complete Segal space equivalent to an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched complete Segal space. Indeed, we can simply complete the underlying
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal category
\tilde{X}
we obtained in
1.78
using the completion construction explained in the last item of
1.83
.

Remark 1.85
.

Unlike
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal spaces, we do not construct any model structure for
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal categories. Indeed, following
[
BR20
, Section 8]
, constructing such a model structure for a general diagram category
\mathcal{D}
might not be possible or at least quite challenging.

1.6.
The Classical Grothendieck Construction

One important step in our work is to generalize the Grothendieck construction to
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories. Hence, we will review it here quickly. The ideas are in no way new and a more detailed approach can be found in many places, such as
[
MLM94
, I.5]
, or
[
Joh02a
, A1.1.7, B1.3.1]
. Our review here is motivated by
[
Ras17b
, Section 1]

Definition 1.86
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category. Define the
Grothendieck construction

\int_{\mathscr{C}}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{C}}

as the functor that takes
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
to the category
\int_{\mathscr{C}}F\to\mathscr{C}
with

•

Objects: Pairs
(c,x)
where
c
is an object in
\mathscr{C}
and
x\in F(c)
.

•

Morphisms: A morphisms
(c,x)\to(d,y)
is a choice of morphism
f:c\to d
in
\mathscr{C}
such that
F(f)(x)=y
.

It comes with an evident projection map
\pi_{F}:\int_{\mathscr{C}}F\to\mathscr{C}
.

This functor has a left adjoint and a right adjoint that we want to define in detail. The key observation is the following example.

Example 1.87
.

Let
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(c,-):\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
be a corepresentable functor. Then
\int_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrm{Hom}(c,-)=\mathscr{C}_{c/}
.

Definition 1.88
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category. We define the functor

\mathscr{C}_{/-}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{C}}

that takes an object to the over-category
\mathscr{C}_{/c}
and a morphism
f:c\to d
to the post-composition
f_{!}:\mathscr{C}_{/c}\to\mathscr{C}_{/d}
.

Similarly, define the functor

\mathscr{C}_{-/}:\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{C}/}

that takes an object to the under-category
\mathscr{C}_{c/}
and a morphism
f:c\to d
to the precomposition
f^{*}:\mathscr{C}_{d/}\to\mathscr{C}_{c/}
.

For a given category over
\mathscr{C}
,
p:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
define

\mathscr{T}_{\mathscr{C}}(p:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}):\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}

as the composition

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\ \mathscr{C}_{/-}\ }\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{C}}\xrightarrow{\ -\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{D}\ }\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{0}\ }\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}

in other words we have
\mathscr{T}_{\mathscr{C}}(c)=\pi_{0}(\mathscr{C}_{/c}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{D})
.
Similarly, define

\mathscr{H}_{\mathscr{C}}(p:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}):\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}

as the composition

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\ (\mathscr{C}_{-/})^{op}\ }(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{C}})^{op}\xrightarrow{\ \mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{C}}(-,\mathscr{D})\ }\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}

meaning we have
\mathscr{H}_{\mathscr{C}}(c)=\mathrm{Hom}_{/\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{C}_{c/},\mathscr{D})
.
We now have the following result.

Proposition 1.89
.

We have the following diagram of adjunctions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int_{\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{H}_{\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{T}_{\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

,

where
\int_{\mathscr{C}}
is fully faithful.

For a proof see
[
Ras17b
, Proposition 1.10, Lemma 1.11]
.

Notice
\int_{\mathscr{C}}
is in fact not essentially surjective, as the following result in
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 1.14]
illustrates. Recall that a functor
p:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
is
conservative
if it reflects isomorphisms.

Lemma 1.90
.

Let
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
be a functor. Then
\pi_{F}:\int_{\mathscr{C}}F\to\mathscr{C}
is conservative.

Restricting our attention to the essential image of
\int_{\mathscr{C}}
leads us to
discrete Grothendieck opfibrations
.

Definition 1.91
.

A functor
P:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
is a
discrete Grothendieck opfibration
over
\mathscr{C}
if it is in the essential image of
\int_{\mathscr{C}}
,
meaning there exists a functor

F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
and isomorphism

\int_{\mathscr{C}}F\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\mathscr{D}

over
\mathscr{C}
.

Fortunately, there is also an internal characterization of discrete Grothendieck opfibrations.

Lemma 1.92
.

A functor
P:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
is a discrete Grothendieck opfibration over
\mathscr{C}
if and only if for
any map
f:C\to C^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}
and object
D
in
\mathscr{D}
such that
P(D)=C
,
there exists a unique lift
\hat{f}:D\to D^{\prime}
such that
P(\hat{f})=f
.

The fact that
\int_{\mathscr{C}}
is fully faithful (
1.89
) implies there is an equivalence of categories between the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
and Grothendieck opfibrations over
\mathscr{C}
. The main goal of
Section

3
is to generalize this equivalence to a homotopical version in the
s\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{D})
-enriched setting.

The equivalence between Grothendieck opfibrations and functors implies the existence of a
universal Grothendieck opfibration
, which is simply the under category projection
\pi_{*}:\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}_{*/}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
. Concretely, we have the following observation:

Proposition 1.93
.

The functor

(-)^{*}\pi_{*}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{C}}

given by pulling back
\pi_{*}
is precisely the functor
\int_{\mathscr{C}}
and so pulling back
\pi_{*}
gives us an equivalence between functors and discrete Grothendieck opfibrations over
\mathscr{C}
.

Given that we can understand
\int_{\mathcal{D}}
via pullback, we would like a version of
1.89
that holds for all categories at once. Notice that discrete Grothendieck opfibrations are stable under pullback as they are characterized via a lifting condition (
1.92
). Hence, we obtain a
pseudo-functor

(1.94)

\mathrm{op}\mathscr{G}\mathrm{roth}_{/-}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}\to\widehat{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}},

that takes a category
\mathscr{C}
to the
large
category of discrete Grothendieck opfibrations over
\mathscr{C}
.

Definition 1.95
.

Let
\mathrm{op}\mathscr{G}\mathrm{roth}
be the category with objects discrete Grothendieck opfibrations and morphisms pullbacks and notice it comes with a projection functor
\mathrm{op}\mathscr{G}\mathrm{roth}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
which takes each fibration to its target.

Concretely, the category and its projection can obtained by applying the pseudo-categorical Grothendieck construction
[
Joh02a
, Theorem B1.3.6]
to the pseudo-functor
1.94
. We now have the following generalization of
1.89
.

Proposition 1.96
.

The pullback functor

(-)^{*}\pi_{*}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{/\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}\to\mathrm{op}\mathscr{G}\mathrm{roth}

is an equivalence over
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
.

Proof.

By
[
Joh02a
, Theorem B1.3.6]
it suffices to prove it is a fiber-wise equivalence, however, this follows immediately from
1.89
and
1.93
.
∎

Remark 1.97
.

Notice,
1.96
in particular implies that for every discrete Grothendieck opfibration
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
, there exists a pullback square of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

,

which is unique up to equivalence.

Finally there is also a contravariant version of this definition, which similarly relates to functors
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
.

Definition 1.98
.

P:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
is called a
discrete Grothendieck fibration
for
any map
f:C\to C^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}
and object
D^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{D}
such that
P(D^{\prime})=C^{\prime}

there exists a
unique
lift
\hat{f}:D\to D^{\prime}
such that
P(\hat{f})=f
.

1.7.
A Reminder on the Covariant Model Structure

Our main goal is to study fibrations of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. The key is to generalize the construction from the case of fibrations of simplicial spaces,
left fibrations
. Left fibrations of simplicial spaces have been studied extensively. Here we will review some key results that we will use throughout and for more details we refer the reader to
[
Ras17b
]
.

A Reedy fibration of simplicial spaces
p:L\to X
(
q:R\to X
) is called a
left fibration
(
right fibration
) if the following is a homotopy pullback square (using
1.21
)

(1.99)

\hbox to100.12pt{\vbox to72.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.05937pt\lower-36.43997pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-50.05937pt}{-28.56053pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.5116pt}{-33.75748pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

There is unique left proper combinatorial simplicial model structure on the over category
s\mathscr{S}_{/X}
, called the
covariant model structure
(
contravariant model structure
)
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.12]
. The study of this model structure and its important properties is the main subject of
[
Ras17b
]
. Here we will only state the relevant properties of the covariant model structure:

(1)

Recognition Principle for Covariant Equivalences

[
Ras17b
, Theorem 4.39]
: A map
f
is a covariant equivalence if and only if for every map
x:F[0]\to X
, if the diagonal of the induced map

Y\underset{X}{\times}R_{x}\to Z\underset{X}{\times}R_{x}

is a Kan equivalence. Here
R_{x}
is the right fibrant replacement of the map
x
over
X
.

(2)

Invariance of Left Fibrations

[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.1]
: Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of simplicial spaces. Then the adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{S}_{/X})^{Cov}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{S}_{/Y})^{Cov}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a Quillen adjunction, which is a Quillen equivalence whenever
f
is a CSS equivalence.

(3)

Left Fibrations are CSS Fibrations

[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.11]
: The following is a Quillen adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{S}_{/X})^{CSS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{S}_{/X})^{Cov}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

where the left hand side has the induced CSS model structure and the right hand side has the covariant model structure.

(4)

Exponentiability

[
Ras17b
, Corollary 5.18]
:
Let
f:X\to Y
be a CSS equivalence and
p:L\to Y
a left fibration over
Y
. Then the map
f^{*}L\to L
is also a CSS equivalence.

There are several results regarding left fibrations that we need, but were not covered in
[
Ras17b
]
, and hence we will be discussed in greater detail.

Definition 1.100
.

Let
p:L\to X
be a map of simplicial spaces. We say
p
is a
left morphism
if for all
n
the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}

is a homotopy pullback square. We similarly define
right morphisms
.

We have the following immediate lemma.

Lemma 1.101
.

Let
p:L\to X
be a left map. Then the Reedy fibrant replacement
\hat{L}\to X
is a left fibration.

There is one key example of a left map.

Example 1.102
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a simplicially enriched category and
c
an object. Define
N\mathscr{C}_{c/}
as the simplicial space with

(N\mathscr{C}_{c/})_{n}=\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{n}}\mathrm{Map}(c,c_{0})\times...\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{n-1},c_{n})

and with simplicial operators given via identity and composition. Then the evident projection map
N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\to N\mathscr{C}
satisfies the condition of a left map, in fact the map
(N\mathscr{C}_{c/})_{n}\to(N\mathscr{C}_{c/})_{0}\times_{N\mathscr{C}_{0}}N\mathscr{C}_{n}
is a bijection of spaces. However, it usually is not a Reedy fibration and so is an example of a left morphism that is not a left fibration.

Although the map
N\mathscr{C}_{/c}\to N\mathscr{C}
is not a right fibration, but only a right morphism we want to prove that in a certain way it does behave like right fibrations.

Lemma 1.103
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a simplicially enriched category and
c
an object. Then the pullback functor

p^{*}:(s\mathscr{S}_{/N\mathscr{C}})^{cov}\to(s\mathscr{S}_{/N\mathscr{C}})^{cov}

is a left Quillen functor between covariant model structures. Here
p
is the map of simplicial spaces
p:N\mathscr{C}_{/c}\to N\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Evidently pullback preserves monomorphisms and so
p^{*}
preserves cofibrations, which are just the level-wise monomorphisms.
Hence, we only need to show that
p^{*}
preserves trivial cofibrations. Let
A\to B
be a trivial Reedy cofibration over
N\mathscr{C}
. We want to show
p^{*}A\to p^{*}B
is a trivial Reedy cofibration over
N\mathscr{C}
. It suffices to prove that
p^{*}A[k]\to p^{*}B[k]
is a Kan equivalence, as we already established it is a cofibration and Reedy equivalences are determined level-wise.

Notice
N\mathscr{C}[0]=\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}
, the set of objects. Hence, we can write
A[0]=\coprod_{c_{0}\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}}A[0]_{c_{0}}
, where
A[0]_{c_{0}}
denotes the subspace of
A[0]
that maps to
c_{0}
. More generally, we have a projection map of spaces
A[k]\to(\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}})^{k+1}
. So we can write
A[k]=\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}A[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}
, where
A[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}
is the subspace of
A[k]
that maps to
(c_{0},...,c_{k})
in
(\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}})^{k+1}
. We can use a similar argument for
B
to conclude that
B[k]=\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}B[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}
and the fact that
A[k]\to B[k]
is an equivalence of spaces over
N\mathscr{C}[0]
implies that
A[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\to B[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}
is an equivalence of spaces for all tuples
(c_{0},...,c_{k})
in
(\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}})^{k+1}
.

Now, by direct computation we have

p^{*}A[k]=\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}(\mathrm{Map}(c_{0},c_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{k-1},c_{k})\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{k},c))\times_{\displaystyle\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\mathrm{Map}(c_{0},c_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{k-1},c_{k})}A[k]=

\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\left((\mathrm{Map}(c_{0},c_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{k-1},c_{k})\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{k},c))\times_{\mathrm{Map}(c_{0},c_{1})\times...\times\mathrm{Map}(c_{k-1},c_{k})}A[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\right)\cong

\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\mathrm{Map}(c_{k},c)\times A[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}

Similarly we have

p^{*}B[k]\cong\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\mathrm{Map}(c_{k},c)\times B[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}

Now the map

\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\mathrm{Map}(c_{k},c)\times A[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\to\coprod_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}\mathrm{Map}(c_{k},c)\times B[k]_{c_{0},...,c_{k}}

is in fact a weak equivalence of spaces, which implies that
p^{*}
preserves trivial Reedy cofibrations, proving that
p^{*}
is left Quillen with respect to the Reedy model structure.

We want to prove that
p^{*}
is in fact left Quillen with respect to the covariant model structure. For that we need to prove that for every map
F[0]\to F[n]\to N\mathscr{C}
, the map
p^{*}F[0]\to p^{*}F[n]
is a covariant equivalence over
N\mathscr{C}
. By induction it suffices to consider the case
\{0\}:F[0]\to F[1]
. So fix a map
F[1]\to N\mathscr{C}
, which corresponds to a morphisms
c_{0}\to c_{1}
. By direct computation
p^{*}F[0]=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(c_{0},c)
as a constant simplicial space. Let
\widehat{p^{*}F[1]}
be the Reedy fibrant replacement of
p^{*}F[1]
and notice
\widehat{p^{*}F[1]}\to F[1]
is a right fibration as
N\mathscr{C}_{/c}\to N\mathscr{C}
is a right map (
1.102
) and by the contravariant analogue to
1.101
. We now have the following diagram of simplicial spaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}F[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}F[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{*}\widehat{p^{*}F[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{p^{*}F[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

.

The vertical morphisms are Reedy equivalences by construction and the bottom map is a covariant equivalence over
F[1]
by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 4.28]
. So, by
2
-out-of-
3
, the top map is a covariant equivalence over
F[1]
and so by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.15]
also a covariant equivalence over
N\mathscr{C}
finishing the proof.
∎

Remark 1.104
.

Notice we could not use
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 4.28]
here directly as the map
N\mathscr{C}_{/c}\to N\mathscr{C}
is not a Reedy fibration (unless
\mathscr{C}
is a simplicially enriched category with discrete mapping spaces) and so in particular not a right fibration (
1.102
).

We need a further result relating over-categories and mapping spaces.

Lemma 1.105
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a simplicially enriched category and
c,d
two objects. Then
N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d}
is a homotopically constant simplicial space and equivalent to the constant simplicial spaces
\mathrm{Map}(c,d)
.

Proof.

By
[
Ras17b
, Thoerem 3.49]
the map
F[0]\to N\mathscr{C}_{c/}
is a covariant equivalence over
N\mathscr{C}
and so, by
1.103
, the induced map

F[0]\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d}\to N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d}
is also a covariant equivalence and so in particular also a diagonal equivalence
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.17]
. By direct computation for every map
{d}:F[0]\to N\mathscr{C}
, we have the equality of constant simplicial spaces

(1.106)

s:F[0]\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{c/}=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(c,d),

proving we have a diagonal equivalence
\mathrm{Map}(c,d)\to N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d}
, meaning the simplicial space is homotopically constant as well.
∎

Remark 1.107
.

Notice the space
(N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d})[k]
has points composable morphisms
c\to c_{0}\to...\to c_{k}\to d
, which can be composed to a morphism
c\to d
. As the composition respects the simplicial identities (given by the identity and composition) this combines into a morphism of spaces
\mathscr{D}\mathrm{iag}(N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d})\to\mathrm{Map}(c,d)
, which, by
1.45
, corresponds to a map of simplicial spaces

p:N\mathscr{C}_{c/}\times_{N\mathscr{C}}N\mathscr{C}_{/d}\to\mathrm{Map}(c,d),

such that
ps:\mathrm{Map}(c,d)\to\mathrm{Map}(c,d)
is the identity map, where
s
is defined in
1.106
, meaning
p
is an equivalence as well.

We will end this section with a review of the
twisted arrow construction
. In
[
BGN18
, Section 2]
the authors describe how to naturally construct a left fibration of quasi-categories
\mathrm{Tw}(S)\to S^{op}\times S
, known as the
twisted arrow construction
. It has the property that the fiber over a point
(x,y)
in
S^{op}\times S
is the mapping space
\mathrm{map}_{S}(x,y)
of the quasi-category. Explicitly it is given by

\mathrm{Tw}(S)_{n}=\mathrm{Hom}_{s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}(\Delta[l]^{op}\ast\Delta[l],S)=\mathrm{Hom}_{s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}(\Delta[2l+1],S)\cong S_{2l+1}.

In the later sections we need the corresponding construction for Segal spaces. In order to motivate things let us give the construction for the analogous complete Segal space. For a given complete Segal space
W
let
\mathrm{Tw}(W)
be the simplicial space given as
\mathrm{Tw}(W)[n]_{l}=\mathrm{Tw}(W[\bullet]_{l})_{n}\cong W[2n+1]_{l}
, meaning we are applying the twisted arrow construction to complete Segal space level-wise. This construction agrees with the quasi-categorical one. Indeed, let
i_{1}^{*}:s\mathscr{S}\to s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
be the right Quillen functor of the Quillen equivalence
(p_{1}^{*},i_{1}^{*})
between quasi-categories and complete Segal spaces defined in
[
JT07
]
. Then
\mathrm{Tw}(i_{1}^{*}(W))=i_{1}^{*}(\mathrm{Tw}(W))
.

Remark 1.108
.

Notice, by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem B.12]
,
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
is a left fibration as the map
i_{1}^{*}(\mathrm{Tw}(W))\to i_{1}^{*}W^{op}\times i_{1}^{*}W
is the twisted arrow construction of the quasi-category
i_{1}^{*}W
. Moreover, the fiber over a point
(x,y)
is the mapping space
\mathrm{map}_{W}(x,y)
.

With this analysis at hand we can now define the twisted arrow construction for Segal spaces.

Definition 1.109
.

For a Segal space
W
define the twisted arrow Segal space
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
as
\mathrm{Tw}(W)[n]_{l}=W[2n+1]_{l}
.

We have the following basic result with regard to the twisted arrow construction for Segal spaces.

Proposition 1.110
.

Let
W
be a Segal space. Then the twisted arrow
\mathrm{Tw}(W)
is also a Segal space and
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
is a left fibration. Moreover, the fiber of
\mathrm{Tw}(W)
over a point
(x,y)
is precisely
\mathrm{map}_{W}(x,y)
. Finally the construction agrees with the one for quasi-categories if
W
is a complete Segal space.

Proof.

We will show
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
is a left fibration. Then
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.11]
implies
\mathrm{Tw}(W)
is a Segal space. Let
W\to\hat{W}
be the completion of the Segal space
W
via a Dwyer-Kan equivalence
[
Rez01
, Section 14]
. Then by construction we have a homotopy pullback square of simplicial spaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tw}(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tw}(\hat{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{op}\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{W}^{op}\times\hat{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

,

which implies that
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
is a left fibration, by
1.108
. Moreover,
1.108
and the fact that
W\to\hat{W}
is a Dwyer-Kan equivalence implies that the fiber of
\mathrm{Tw}(W)
over a point
(x,y)
in
W^{op}\times W
is
\mathrm{map}_{W}(x,y)
.
Finally, if
W
is complete, then the twisted arrow construction coincides with the one for quasi-categories with
i_{1}^{*}
.
∎

2.
From Left Fibrations to
\mathcal{D}
-left Fibrations

In this section we define
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations (
2.1
) and give several alternative characterizations. We then move on to show that
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations are fibrant objects in a model structure (
2.12
) and study some basic properties of this model structure. The key idea is to think of a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space as a
\mathcal{D}
-space (the values) multiplied with a simplicial space (the functoriality). Hence we need to impose the appropriate condition on an injective fibration to make this intuition precise. We end with an analysis of
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over level-wise Segal spaces and in particular the Yoneda lemma (
2.31
).

2.1.
Introducing
\mathcal{D}
-Left Fibrations

Let us start with a definition and its various alternatives.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
p:Y\to X
be an injective fibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. Then
p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration
if for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, the map
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a left fibration of simplicial spaces.

We can generalize the definition of a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration similar to
1.100
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
p:L\to X
be a map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. We say
p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left morphism
if for all object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}L\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a left morphism.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
p:L\to X
be a
\mathcal{D}
-left map. Then the injective fibrant replacement
\hat{L}\to X
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Our first task is to give several alternative characterizations of
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations generalizing results in
[
Ras17b
]
. In order to be able to make appropriate inductive arguments we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
P
be a property about
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. We say the property
P

is level-wise
if an injectively fibrant
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space satisfies
P
if and only if for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
satisfies property
P
.

Combining
2.1
and
2.4
we immediately have the following useful result.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
p:Y\to X
be an injective fibration. Then being a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is a level-wise property.

We can now combine results from
[
Ras17b
]
with
2.5
for many interesting results.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
p:Y\to X
be an injective fibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. The following are equivalent:

(1)

p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

(2)

For each
(d,k)
in
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
, the map

(p[d,k],<0>^{*}):Y[d,k]\to X[d,k]\underset{X[d,0]}{\times}Y[d,0]

is a Kan equivalence.

(3)

For each
n\geq 0
, the map

(p[d,k],<0>^{*}):Y[d,k]\to X[d,k]\underset{X[d,0]}{\times}Y[d,0]

is a trivial Kan fibration.

Proof.

Follows from combining
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.5]
and the fact that being a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is level-wise (
2.5
).
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
p:Y\to X
be an injective fibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. The following two are equivalent:

(1)

The commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y[d,k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y[d,0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p[d,k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p[d,0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}

is a homotopy pullback square for all
(d,k)
in
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
, meaning
p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

(2)

The commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y[d,k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y[d,k-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,k-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0,...,k-1>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p[d,k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p[d,k-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0,...,k_{n}-1>^{*}}

is a homotopy pullback square for all
(d,k)
in
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
.

Proof.

Again we use the fact that being a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is level-wise (
2.5
) and
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.6]
.
∎

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
p:Y\to X
be an injective fibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

(2)

For every object
(d,k)
in
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
, the map
p
satisfies the right lifting property with respect to maps

(<0>:F[d,0]\to F[d,k])\square(\partial\Delta[l]\to\Delta[l]).

Proof.

By definition
p:Y\to X
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration if and only if for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of simplicial spaces
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}p:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a left fibration. By
[
Ras17b
, Proposition 3.7]
, this is equivalent to

(F[0]\to F[n])\square(\partial\Delta[l]\to\Delta[l])\pitchfork\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}p.

By the adjunction
1.38
/
1.44
, this is equivalent to
is equivalent to

\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nc}_{d}((F[0]\to F[n])\square(\partial\Delta[l]\to\Delta[l]))\pitchfork p,

which by direct computation gives us

(F[d,0]\to F[d,n])\square(\partial\Delta[l]\to\Delta[l])\pitchfork p,

finishing the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
f:Y\to X
and
g:Z\to Y
be two injective fibrations.

(1)

If
f
and
g
are
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations then
fg
is also a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

(2)

If
f
and
fg
are
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations then
g
is also a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Proof.

We can apply
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.8]
to the level-wise property of
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations (
2.5
).
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

The pullback of a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Proof.

Follows from combining
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.9]
and the level-wise property of
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations (
2.5
).
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
p:L\to X
be an injective fibration. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

p
is an
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

(2)

For every map
F[d,k]\times\Delta[l]\to X
the pullback
L\times_{X}(F[d,k]\times\Delta[l])\to F[d,k]\times\Delta[l]
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

(3)

For every map
F[d,k]\to X
the pullback
L\times_{X}F[d,k]\to F[d,k]
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Proof.

We simply have to adapt the proof of
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.10]
as we cannot use the level-wise argument immediately. It is immediate that
(1)
implies
(3)
. Now we want to prove that condition
(2)
implies that
p
is an
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration. By
2.8
it suffices to check that it satisfies the right lifting property with respect to maps
F[k_{1},...,k_{n}]\times\partial\Delta[l]\coprod_{F[k_{1},...,k_{n-1},0]\times\partial\Delta[l]}F[k_{1},...,k_{n-1},0]\times\Delta[l]\to F[k_{1},...,k_{n}]\times\Delta[l]
. We now have the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle F[d,k]\times\partial\Delta[l]\coprod_{F[d,0]\times\partial\Delta[l]}F[d,0]\times\Delta[l]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[d,k]\times\Delta[l]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F[d,k]\times\Delta[l]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

.

The left hand square has a lift by assumption and so
p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration. Finally, we want to prove that
(3)
implies
(2)
, but this condition is level-wise and so follows from
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.10]
combined with
2.5
.
∎

2.2.
The
\mathcal{D}
-Covariant Model Structure

We are now in the right position to define a model structure, generalizing
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.12]
.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
X
be an
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space.
There is a unique combinatorial left proper model structure enriched in
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})^{inj}
with the injective model structure on the over-category
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X}
, called the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure and denoted by
(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}
, which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

The fibrant objects are the
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over
X
.

(2)

Cofibrations are monomorphisms.

(3)

A map
f:A\to B
is a weak equivalence if it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions:

•

For all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the induced map

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}f:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}A\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}B

is a covariant equivalence of simplicial spaces over
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
.

•

For all
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations
L\to X
the induced map

\mathrm{Map}_{/X}(B,L)\to\mathrm{Map}_{/X}(A,L)

is a Kan equivalence of spaces.

(4)

A weak equivalence (
\mathcal{D}
-covariant fibration) between fibrant objects is a level-wise equivalence (injective fibration).

Proof.

We can use the theory of Bousfield localizations with respect to the injective model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X}
and localize with respect to

\mathcal{L}=\{F[d,0]\to F[d,k]\to X:d\in\mathcal{D},k\geq 0\}.

We want to prove the resulting model structure satisfies all the conditions stated above. By the definition of Bousfield localizations the resulting model structure is simplicial, combinatorial, left proper with cofibrations monomorphisms. Hence we need to verify the remaining properties.

Let us first confirm the fibrations. Let
p:L\to X
be an injective fibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. Then, by
2.6
,
p
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration if for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
and
n\geq 0
the map of spaces is a Kan equivalence

L[d,n]\to X[d,n]\times_{X[d,0]}L[d,0].

We can extend this map to a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[d,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,n]\times_{X[d,0]}L[d,0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,n]}

,

where two diagonal maps are Kan fibrations. By a classical result for Kan fibrations (
[
Rez17
, Section 38]
), the top map is an equivalence if and only if it is a fiber-wise equivalence, meaning the map

L[d,n]\times_{X[d,n]}\Delta[0]\to\Delta[0]\times_{X[d,n]}X[d,n]\times_{X[d,0]}L[d,0]\cong\Delta[0]\times_{X[d,0]}L[d,0]

is a Kan equivalence for all points in
X[d,n]
. By the Yoneda lemma this is equivalent to the map of spaces

\mathrm{Map}_{/X}(F[d,n],L)\to\mathrm{Map}_{/X}(F[d,0],L)

being a Kan equivalence, which is the definition of fibrant objects in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure.

We move on to characterize weak equivalences. Let
Y\to X
be an arbitrary map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. Recall that we obtain the fibrant replacement in the localization model structure by applying the small object argument with respect to maps of the following form:

•

(A\to B)\square(\Lambda[l]_{i}\to\Delta[l])\to X
,

•

(F[d,0]\to F[d,n])\square(\partial\Delta[l]\to\Delta[l])\to X
,

where
A\to B
is a injective cofibration. This means we obtain a fibrant replacement of
Y\to X
in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure via a chain of morphisms

Y=Y_{0}\to Y_{1}\to...\to\hat{Y}

over
X
, where each morphism is a pushout along the two classes of morphisms of the form above. As both of these classes of morphisms are level-wise covariant equivalences, this means the map
Y\to\hat{Y}
is also a level-wise covariant equivalence over
X
.

We will now use that to characterize arbitrary equivalences. Let
f:Y\to Z
be an arbitrary map over
X
and let
\hat{f}:\hat{Y}\to\hat{Z}
be the fibrant replacement. By definition,
f
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence if and only if
\hat{f}
is an injective equivalence, which is equivalent to
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{f}:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Y}\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Z}
being a Reedy equivalence of simplicial spaces for every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. For every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
we have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}}

.

By the argument above, the maps
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Y}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Z\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Z}
are covariant equivalences, hence
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Y}\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\hat{Z}
is a Reedy equivalence if and only if
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Z
is a covariant equivalence over
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
, which proves that a map
Y\to Z
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence if and only if it is a level-wise covariant equivalence over
X
.

Finally, we want to show that the covariant model structure is enriched over
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})^{inj}
via the inclusion
\mathscr{V}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{mb}:s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})
. Let
i:A\to B
be a cofibration of
\mathcal{D}
-spaces and
j:C\to D
over
X
be a cofibration of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. By
1.7
, we need to prove that
i\square j
is a cofibration over
X
, which is trivial if either
i
or
j
is trivial. The cofibration part is evident as as all cofibrations are monomorphisms, thus we only need to check the second part. Assume that either
i
or
j
are trivial cofibrations. Then
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}i
or
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}j
are trivial cofibrations for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. This implies that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(i\square j)=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}j\square\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}j
is a trivial cofibration for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, which implies that
i\square j
is a trivial cofibrations in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

There is a certain similarity between the way we constructed the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure and the level-wise localized model structures (
1.50
), which might suggest we could have just constructed the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure as a certain level-wise localized model structure. Unfortunately this approach does not work. Indeed, although we have a level-wise covariant model structure, each level is over a different base (
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
) and so the different levels do not have the same model structure.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
p:L\to X
and
q:L^{\prime}\to X
be two
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations. A map
f:L\to L^{\prime}
over
X
is a fibration in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant
model structure if and only if it is an
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Proof.

Being a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is a level-wise condition (
2.5
) and so it follows from
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.14]
.
∎

Theorem 2.15
.

The following is a Quillen adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-diag}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

which is a Quillen equivalence if for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the simplicial space
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is homotopically constant. Here the left side has the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure and the right side has the
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal model structure (
1.51
) on the over category. This implies that the
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal model structure over
X
is a localization of the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure over
X
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the left adjoint preserves cofibrations and trivial cofibrations. However, by
2.12
, both are determined level-wise (
2.4
) and so the result follows from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.17]
. Now let us assume
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is homotopically constant for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. We want to prove that the left adjoint reflects equivalences and the derived counit map is an equivalence.

By
2.12
, every object is cofibrant and so the derived counit map is simply the counit map. Hence, both conditions (left adjoint reflecting equivalences and the counit being an equivalence) are determined level-wise (
2.4
) and so the result follows from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.17]
combined with the fact that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is homotopically constant.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
p:L\to X
be an
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration and
i:A\to B
,
j:C\to D
monomorphisms.

(1)

If
i
or
j
are
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalences then
i\square j
is a trivial
\mathcal{D}
-covariant cofibration.

(2)

\mathrm{exp}(i,p)
is an
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Proof.

As coproducts, products, monomorphisms and weak equivalences are determined level-wise (
2.4
) the first statement follows from
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.25]
applied level-wise. The second one then follows from the first by
1.5
.
∎

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
i:A\to B
be a monomorphism of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces over
X
. Then
i
is a trivial cofibration in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure on
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X}
if there exists a retraction
r:B\to A
(not necessarily over
X
) and a
H:B\times D[1]\to B
such that

H(-,0)=ir
and
H(-,1)=\mathrm{id}_{B}
.

Proof.

i:A\to B
being a trivial cofibration is determined level-wise (
2.4
) and so the result follows from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.27]
.
∎

We can appropriately relate the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure and the level-wise complete Segal space model structure (
1.59
).

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. Then the adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/Y})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Quillen adjunction, which is a Quillen equivalence whenever
f
is a level-wise CSS equivalence (
1.59
). Here both sides have the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure.

Proof.

By
1.26
the adjunction is evidently
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched as post-composition preserves tensors. It is a Quillen adjunction as
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations are closed under pullbacks (
2.10
) and both sides have the same cofibrations (inclusions). So, let us assume
f
is a level-wise CSS equivalence. We want to prove that
(f_{!}\dashv f^{*})
is a Quillen equivalence. We will prove that the derived counit is an equivalence and that the left adjoint reflects equivalences.

By
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.1]
the derived counit map is a level-wise covariant equivalence and so an equivalence in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure (
2.12
). On the other hand assume
A\to B
a map over
X
such that it is an
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence over
Y
. Fix an element
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. Then, again, by
2.12
,
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}A\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}B
is a covariant equivalence over
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y
and so, by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.1]
, is a covariant equivalence over
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
, which, again by
2.12
, implies that
A\to B
is an
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence over
X
.
∎

Theorem 2.19
.

Let
X
be a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space. Then the following adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-CSS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathscr{D}))_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a Quillen adjunction.
Here the left hand side has the level-wise CSS model structure (
1.59
) on the over-category and the right hand side has the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure.

This implies that the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure over
X
is a localization of the induced level-wise CSS model structure over
X
.

Proof.

Evidently the left adjoint preserves cofibrations as they are simply the inclusions. Hence it suffices to show that the right adjoint preserves fibrant objects, meaning we have to show every
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration is a level-wise complete Segal space fibration. As both conditions are level-wise (
2.4
) it follows from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.11]
.
∎

Remark 2.20
.

Analogous to left fibrations, which can be dualized and give us
right fibrations
,
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations can be dualized to
\mathcal{D}
-right fibrations
. Concretely, a
\mathcal{D}
-right fibration is an injective fibration
p:Y\to X
such that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}:\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}Y\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}X
is a right fibration for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. We can use this definition to repeat all the results we have stated up until now regarding
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations for
\mathcal{D}
-right fibrations. In particular,
\mathcal{D}
-right fibrations are also the fibrant objects in a model structure, the
\mathcal{D}
-contravariant model structure
.

We only state the following lemma explicitly connecting
\mathcal{D}
-left and
\mathcal{D}
-right fibrations.

Lemma 2.21
.

A map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
p:L\to X
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration if and only if
p^{op}:L^{op}\to X^{op}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-right fibration,
(-)^{op}
is given in
1.34
.

Proof.

It is immediate
p
is an injective fibration if and only if
p^{op}
is an injective fibration. Now, for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
,
(<0>^{*})^{op}:L[d,n]\to L[d,0]
is equal to
<n>^{*}:L^{op}[d,n]\to L^{op}[d,0]
, which implies that in the following two commutative squares
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[d,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[d,0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{op}[d,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{op}[d,0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X[d,0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{op}[d,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{op}[d,0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p[d,n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p[d,0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<n>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{op}[d,n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{op}[d,0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<n>^{*}}

one is a homotopy pullback square if and only if the other one is a homotopy pullback square and hence we are done.
∎

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
p:R\to X
be a
\mathcal{D}
-right or
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration. Then the adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{lev_{CSS}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/R})^{lev_{CSS}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a Quillen adjunction. Here both sides have the level-wise CSS model structure (
1.59
) on the over-category.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the left adjoint preserves cofibrations and trivial cofibrations. Both of those are determined level-wise (
2.4
) and so the statement follows from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 5.15]
.
∎

Theorem 2.23
.

Let
p:R\to X
be a
\mathcal{D}
-right fibration. Then the adjunction
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})_{/X})^{\mathcal{D}-cov}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

is a Quillen adjunction where both sides have the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the left adjoint preserves cofibrations and trivial cofibrations. However, being a (trivial) cofibration in the
\mathcal{D}
-covariant model structure is a level-wise property (
2.4
). Hence, this follows from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 4.28]
.
∎

We can use these results to give various characterizations of
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalences between
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations.

Theorem 2.24
.

Let
L\to X
and
L^{\prime}\to X
be
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations and
f:L\to L^{\prime}
a map over
X
.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

f
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence.

(2)

f
is an injective equivalence.

(3)

f
is a
\mathcal{D}
-value equivalence (
1.52
).

(4)

f
is a fiberwise injective equivalence (
\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}f:\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}L\to\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}L^{\prime}
is an injective equivalence for all maps
x:F[d,0]\to X
).

(5)

f
is a fiberwise
\mathcal{D}
-value equivalence (
\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}f:\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}L\to\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}L^{\prime}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-value equivalence for all maps
x:F[d,0]\to X
).

(6)

f
is a fiberwise
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence (
\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}f:\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}L\to\mathscr{F}\mathrm{ib}_{x}L^{\prime}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence for all maps
x:F[d,0]\to X
).

Proof.

The six conditions are all analogues of the six conditions in
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 4.34]
, hence it suffices to confirm that they are all determined level-wise (
2.4
). The fact that
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalences are level-wise follows from
2.12
, that injective equivalences are level-wise from
1.46
, that the
\mathcal{D}
-value model structure equivalences are level-wise from
1.52
and finally that the
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal model structure equivalences are level-wise from
1.51
.
∎

Theorem 2.25
.

Let
g:Y\to Z
be a map over
X
and
R_{x}\to X
a choice of
\mathcal{D}
-contravariant fibrant replacement of the map
\{x\}:F[d,0]\to X
.
Then
g:Y\to Z
over
X
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence if and only if for every
\{x\}:F[d,0]\to X

R_{x}\underset{X}{\times}Y\to R_{x}\underset{X}{\times}Z

is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence. Here
R_{x}\to X
is the fibrant replacement of
x
in the
\mathcal{D}
-contravariant model structure.

Proof.

Let
\hat{g}:\hat{Y}\to\hat{Z}
be a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrant replacement. By
2.12
,
g:Y\to Z
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence if and only if
\hat{g}:\hat{Y}\to\hat{Z}
is an injective equivalence. We now have the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\underset{X}{\times}R_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\underset{X}{\times}R_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{Y}\underset{X}{\times}R_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{Z}\underset{X}{\times}R_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{Y}\underset{X}{\times}F[d,0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{Z}\underset{X}{\times}F[d,0]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{g}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{g}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

.

By
2.23
, the two top vertical maps are
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalences and by the dual of
2.23
(explained in
2.20
) the two bottom vertical maps are
\mathcal{D}
-contravariant equivalences.
Hence, by
2.15
and
2.20
, all vertical maps are
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalences and so the top horizontal map
is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence if and only if the bottom horizontal map is one.
But the bottom map is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence for every
x:F[d,0]\to X
if and only if
\hat{Y}\to\hat{Z}
is an injective equivalence (by
2.24
) and hence we are done.
∎

Remark 2.26
.

Notice, the proof of
2.25
cannot be immediately reduced to a level-wise proof and so we cannot simply cite the proof in
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 4.39]
, as the property of being a fibrant replacement is not necessarily a level-wise property.

In certain situations we can simplify the conditions in
2.25
.

Corollary 2.27
.

Let
X
be a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space with weakly constant objects. Then a map of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
g:Y\to Z
over
X
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence if and only if for every map
\{x\}:D[0]\to X
,

R_{x}\underset{X}{\times}Y\to R_{x}\underset{X}{\times}Z

is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence.

Proof.

The condition given here is a special case of
2.25
and so we only need to prove it suffices when
X
has weakly constant objects. Let
X\to\hat{X}\leftarrow\tilde{X}
be the zigzag given in
1.78
. The fact that both maps are complete Segal space equivalences implies that any map
F[d,0]\to X
is equivalent to a map
F[d,0]\to\tilde{X}
(by
2.18
). Now, by assumption
X
has weakly constant objects, which means, by
1.78
, that
\tilde{X}
has discrete objects and the map factors as
F[d,0]\to D[0]\xrightarrow{\{x\}}\tilde{X}
, where
x
is the image of the map from
D[0]
.

We can post-compose and, by
2.12
,
F[d,0]\cong F[d,0]\times D[0]\to F[d,0]\times\hat{R}_{x}
is the fibrant replacement of
F[d,0]\to D[0]\to\tilde{X}\to\hat{X}
. By
2.18
, this means
F[d,0]\times R_{x}=F[d,0]\times i^{*}\hat{R}_{x}
is the fibrant replacement of
F[d,0]
in the
\mathcal{D}
-contravariant model structure over
X
.

Now by direct computation, for a given map
Y\to Z
the map

(F[d,0]\times R_{x})\times_{X}Y\to(F[d,0]\times R_{x})\times_{X}Z

is isomorphic to

F[d,0]\times(R_{x}\times_{X}Y)\to F[d,0]\times(R_{x}\times_{X}Z),

which is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence if and only if
R_{x}\times_{X}Y\to R_{x}\times_{X}Z
is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence.
∎

2.3.
Fibrations over Segal Spaces and the Yoneda Lemma

In this section we focus on
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations over level-wise Segal spaces (
1.55
).

Definition 2.28
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space and
\{x\}:D[0]\to W
be a map and define
W_{x/}
via the pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{x/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{D[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<0>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

which we call the
level-wise under-Segal space
.

The name suggests the resulting
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space is in fact a level-wise Segal space.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal and
x:D[0]\to W
, then
W_{x/}
is also a level-wise Segal space.

Proof.

By direct computation we have

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W_{x/}=(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W)^{F[1]}\times_{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W}F[0]=(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W)_{x/}

which is a Segal space as
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(W)
is a Segal space (
1.56
) combined with
[
Ras17b
, Lemma 3.43]
.
∎

Theorem 2.30
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space and
x:D[0]\to W
. Then the projection map
W_{x/}\to W
is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration.

Proof.

The injective model structure is Cartesian and so the map
W^{D[1]}\to W\times W
is an injective fibration and so the pullback
W_{x/}\to W
is also an injective fibration. Hence, it suffices to prove that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W_{x/}\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W
is a left fibration for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. By direct computation this map is simply equal to

(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W)_{x/}\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(W),

which is a left fibration by
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.44]
.
∎

Theorem 2.31
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space and
x
an object. Then the map
\{id_{x}\}:D[0]\to W_{x/}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence over
W
.

Proof.

The property of being a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence is level-wise (
2.4
), which means it suffices to prove that
F[0]\to(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W)_{x/}
is a covariant equivalence over
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(W)
for all objects
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. This fact follows immediately from
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.49]
.
∎

Remark 2.32
.

Notice we could have proven
2.31
directly using
2.17
and a diagram similar to the one given in the proof of
[
Ras17b
, Theorem 3.49]
.

There is an analogous contravariant version.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space and
\{x\}:D[0]\to W
be a map and define
W_{/x}
via the pullback
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{/x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{D[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces<1>^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

which we call the
level-wise over-Segal space
.

Similar to
2.30

W_{/x}\to W
is a right fibration and similar to
2.31

D[0]\to W_{/x}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-contravariant equivalence over
W
.

The construction of the over-Segal space only depends on a morphism
D[0]\to W
and hence suggest that we should focus on attention on
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces with weakly constant objects (
1.66
). Let
\mathcal{D}
have a terminal object
t
and let
W
be an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space. Then a map
D[0]\to W
is precisely a choice of object in
W
(
1.68
) and
2.27
takes the following form.

Corollary 2.34
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category with terminal object.
Let
W
be an
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched Segal space. Then a morphism of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces
Y\to Z
over
W
is a
\mathcal{D}
-covariant equivalence if and only if for every object
x
in
W
, the map

Y\times_{W}W_{/x}\to Z\times_{W}W_{/x}

is a
\mathcal{D}
-diagonal equivalence.

We can generalize the construction from slice categories (
2.28
) to
twisted arrow categories
. For a level-wise Segal space
W
define
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\to W
level-wise as the twisted arrow construction given in
1.109
. Here
W^{op}
is the opposite
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial space described in
1.34
.

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space and let
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
be the level-wise twisted arrow construction. Then the map is a
\mathcal{D}
-left fibration, with fiber over two objects
x,y
the mapping
\mathcal{D}
-space
\mathrm{map}_{W}(x,y)
(
1.69
).

Proof.

For every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, we have
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathrm{Tw}(W)=\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}W)
and so result from the level-wise characterization of
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations and mapping
\mathcal{D}
-spaces.
∎

We want to give one last example of a fibration in the level-wise Segal setting, however, this requires us to expand the playing field.

Example 2.36
.

Let
W
be a level-wise Segal space. Then we can take
W
as a constant
\mathcal{D}
-bisimplicial space i.e. as a constant simplicial object in
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces. In this setting the map
W^{op}\times W\to W
is a
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
-left fibration, which follows from applying
2.15
to the
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
-covariant model structure.

On the other side, we can also take
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W
as a map of constant simplicial objects in
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces, and, by
2.35
, this is also a
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
-left fibration. Hence, by
2.9
, the composition
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W
is a
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
-left fibration. For a given object
x
, the fiber is the level-wise under-Segal space
W_{x/}
, hence we call it the
target fibration
.

Example 2.37
.

Using the same arguments as in
2.36
we can deduce that
\mathrm{Tw}(W)\to W^{op}\times W\to W^{op}
is a
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
-left fibration and so, by
2.21
,
\mathrm{Tw}(W)^{op}\to W
is a
\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
-right fibration, which has fiber over a point
x
the level-wise over Segal space
W_{/x}
and is hence called the
source fibration
.

3.
Grothendieck Construction for
\mathcal{D}
-Simplicial Spaces

In this section we want to present the Grothendieck construction in the
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched setting, relating functors and fibrations. We will proceed in three steps each generalizing the previous step.

(1)

A strict Grothendieck construction for categories, relating
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
with
\mathcal{D}
-Grothendieck opfibrations
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
, where
\mathscr{C}
is a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category (
Section

3.1
).

(2)

A homotopical Grothendieck construction for categories, relating
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors
\mathscr{C}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
with
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations
L\to N_{\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{C}
, where
\mathscr{C}
is a
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category (
Section

3.2
).

(3)

A homotopical Grothendieck construction for
\mathcal{D}
-spaces with weakly constant objects, relating
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor
\mathscr{C}_{X}\to s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
with
\mathcal{D}
-left fibrations
L\to X
, where
X
is a
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial spaces with weakly constant objects and
\mathscr{C}_{X}
is its associated
s\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category (
Section

3.3
).

3.1.
Grothendieck Construction for
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-Enriched Categories

In this subsection we generalize the classical Grothendieck construction from (set-enriched) categories (
Section

1.6
) to
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories and we then use to construct the desired Quillen equivalence.

Remark 3.1
.

There is an enriched Grothendieck construction due to Beardsley and Wong
[
BW19
]
, which holds for more general enrichments, however, only over free enriched categories, whereas we need the Grothendieck construction over arbitrary
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories.

Recall that the category of
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets is enriched over itself (
1.22
) and so for a category enriched over
\mathcal{D}
-simplicial sets
\mathscr{C}
we can define the category of enriched functors
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))
(
1.23
). We would like to study this category in terms of unenriched functors.

Definition 3.2
.

A
\mathcal{D}
-category
is a functor
\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
. We denote the category of
\mathcal{D}
-categories by
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})
and notice it is enriched and tensored over
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
, with tensor given by applying
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},-)
to the tensor of
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}\times\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a fully faithful (set-enriched) functor

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})

which takes a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
to the
\mathcal{D}
-category
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{C}):\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
, which takes an element
d
to the category
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{C}
with
\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(\mathscr{C})}=\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}
and morphisms given by

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}(\mathscr{C})}(x,y)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)[d].

Its essential image given by functors
Q:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
such that for all morphisms
f:d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
the induced map
P(f):P(d^{\prime})\to P(d)
is the identity on objects.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}),\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
be the functor that takes an object
d
to the representable functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(F[d],-):\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
, which corresponds to evaluating a presheaf
P:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
at
d
, giving us
P(d)
. Notice representable functors are product preserving and so we can restrict the codomain to
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathrm{Fun}^{\times}(\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}),\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})
. We can now post-compose this functor with
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{h}
(
1.17
) to define a functor

\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathrm{Fun}^{\times}(\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}),\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}).

Using the adjunction between product and functor categories the data of this functor is equivalent to a functor

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}).

We now want to prove it is fully faithful and characterize the essential image. To observe it is fully faithful note we have the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},N)}

.

The left hand functor is fully faithful by
1.37
and the right hand functor is fully faithful as the nerve is fully faithful.
The fact that the two diagonal maps are fully faithful implies that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}
is fully faithful as well. Finally the characterization of the essential image follows from the construction of
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}
.
∎

Example 3.4
.

By
1.22
, the category
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
is
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched and the category
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
has objects
\mathcal{D}
-sets and set of morphisms

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(X,Y)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(X,Y)[d]=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(X\times F[d],Y).

This lemma gives us the following important identification.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category.
There is an isomorphism of categories

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{C}),\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}))).

The name
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}
should remind us of the definition of the underlying simplicial set (
1.38
). In fact for a given
d
in
\mathcal{D}
we have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}}

.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category.
A
\mathcal{D}
-Grothendieck opfibration
over
\mathscr{C}
is a map of
\mathcal{D}
-categories
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}\mathscr{C}
such that for all
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, the functor
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{C}
is a discrete Grothendieck opfibration of categories (
1.91
).

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched category.
Let
\mathrm{op}\mathscr{G}\mathrm{roth}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathscr{C}}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{op},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})_{/\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}\mathscr{C}}
consisting of
\mathcal{D}
-Grothendieck opfibrations.

We want to construct an adjunction that induces an equivalence between
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functors of
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched categories
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
and
\mathcal{D}
-Grothendieck opfibrations over
\mathscr{C}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
be a
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
-enriched functor. For each object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
, define the set-enriched functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(D[0],\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}F):\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}
as the composition

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}F}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}_{d}\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})}(D[0],-)}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{et}

Now, define the
\mathcal{D}
-Grothendieck opfibration
\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}/\mathscr{C}}F\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{nd}\mathscr{C}
as follows. It takes an object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
to the Grothendieck opfibration
